WORKING PLAN 


OF THE 


TEHRI-GARHWAL LEASED FORESTS, 
JAUNSAR FOREST DIVISION, 
NORTH-WESTERN PROVINCES AND OUDH. 


By N. HEARLE, 


DEPUTY OCONSERVATOR OF FORESTS, SOHOOL CIROLE, 


ALLAHABAD : 


NORTH-WESTERN PROVINCES AND OUDH GOVERNMENT PRESS. 


1888, 


INTRODUCTION. 


It has been found that deoddr forests whose resources are drawn on 
for the first time, and where costly export works are required, can only 
be worked remuneratively on a large scale, and that, in consequence, 
large working circles have to be formed. The present plan, thérefore, 
when it prescribes that the Tehri leased forests, with the exception of the 
Lambatdéch and Datmir forests, shall be worked through in 20 years, 
regards them as forming a portion only of a‘large working cirele, the 
precise composition of which cannot be determined until the remaining 
deodér forests in the Jaunsdr Division have been valued. In fact, there 
secms no good reason for treating them apart from the other forests, as 
the British Government has aright to renew the lease fora further 
terth at the expiry of the present lease in 1905, 


It may be useful here to state my views regarding the working of 
the Jaunsdr deodar forests as a whole, subject, of course, to considerable 
modification when all the forests have been valued and their resources 
thoroughly well kuown. The forests which remain to be valued are 
Lambatach, Dadi, Raiengarh, Chachpur, Mordch, Koti (Bawar), Kathidu, 
Riknar, Bomnai, Majbira, Sangrdni, Lokar, Jako and Haridnta aggregat- 
ing about 20,000 acres, or one season’s work for a Working Plans’ officer. 


Four working circles will probably be formed asin the following

The Jaunsfr-Bawar export forests, with the exception of Mundahi, 
have all been more or less worked for many years past. Timber from 
Moradch and Koti (Bawar) was exported vid the Tons before the Tehri 
forests were tapped, whilst the forests near Deoban have contributed to 
the timber supply required for Chakrata, and for free grants. The latter 
forests were in a semi-ruined state when taken charge of by the Forest 
Department, and although they have improved immensely, it will not be 
possible to work them for export purposes for many years to come. 


The Karamba forest has been only slightly indented on by free grantees 
and contains many very fine trees. It should suffice to supply Chakrata 
with timber for the next 20 or 30 years, after which Konain and Rikta or 
Konain alone would probably furnish enough timber, provided the station 
had not been considerably enlarged in the meantime. 


Little is known about the resources of the forests it is proposed to 
set aside for free grants, but itis probable that they will more than 
uffice for local requirements, aided as they must be by the other second- 
class forests, because the villages adjoining such forests must obtain their 
trees from them. 


As regards the leased forests, Raiengarh has been worked out, and 
it is prebable that Didi, Chachpur, Datmir and Lambatdch (last worked 
in 1875), together with portions of forest reserved in paragraph 120 of 
of this plan, and which may escape being worked during the term of the 
lease, will supply enough timber for the first period of the rotation. If 
the valuation surveys to be made show that this will not be the case, 
then it may be perhaps better to. form one working circle for the leased 
forests and the Jaunsdr-Bawar export forests combined, or to add to the 
former Mordch, Koti (Biwar) and Kathidn according to, the results of 
the enumeration. : 


It is clear from this that the deoddr forests of the division are being 
worked very prudently, and that the proper time for working the 
Mundali forest caunot well be decided until the remaining deoddr areas 
have been examined and valued. 


Exceptional difficulties had to be overcome in the preparation of the 
present plan. More than half the forests had to be demarcated and rough 
sketch maps of them prepared ; at the same time that they were divided 
into blocks and compartments, and a complete enumeration made of 
their contents in deodér. The compartments had also to be described 
simultaneously with all this work, whilst the physical obstacles to contend 
with were of no mean order : the ground being often precipitous, fre- 
quently inaccessible, and, as a rule, there were scarcely any foot-tracks. 


( 3 ) 


The descriptions are not therefore very accurate, and are often wanting 
in detail ; but the difficulties met with will, it is hoped, be considered a 
sufficient excuse for these shortcomings. They were prepared before 
the views of the Inspector-General of Forests regarding co-efficients were 
circulated, or such figures would have been omitted. 


‘Lhe following reports, &c., have been of great use in preparing the 
plan : Suggestions by Sir D. Brandis, 1879 and 1881; Deota sledge road, 
by Mr. E, McA.Moir; Annual Reports of Forest Administration; North- 
Western Provinces and Qudh ; and Plantation Notes by Forest Students,

CHAPTER L 


SITUATION, BOUNDARIES, AREA AND HISTORY. 


1. These leased forests are all situated in the upper valley of the river Tons 
and on its right bunk with the exception of compartment 1, Datmir, on the left bank. 
There are five separately-demarcated areas, and the first three are conterminous, form- 
ing perhaps the largest and most compact deodér forest in the world. Their bound- 


aries are— 

2. (1)—Deota forests (including Lambat&éch, Deota, Bamsu, Saris and tho Koti- 
gad forests, except the Gokal block). 

On the north a line demarcated by pillars from a point (pillar No. 28) on the 
Paji dh&ér above the Bargat g4d, passing south of the village lands of Makuri, toa 
point on the Damiéri g&d (pillar No. 47) ; on the east a line from the last-mentioned 
point passing above the Jajrari, Bumeu and Deota villages to the Bhagiadr stream, 
half a mile above its junction with the Tons; on the south from the last-men- 
tioned point a demarcated line running above the old Kiméri and Pokhari villages 
to a point on the Lohdsu dbér (pillar No. 11) ; on the west from the last-mcationed 
point a demarcated line skirting the Karoli, Maingani, Bhutér and Dhdmti villages 
to the point on the Paji dhar (pillar-No. 28). 

8. (2)—The Chfnsil forests (including the Chansil and Séhlra forests and the 
Gokal block of the Kotigad forost). 

The boundary runs in a generally south-westerly direction from pillar No, 1 
just below the Kuamund peak on the main Chénsil ridge down the Galtai gAd to its 
junction with the Dedra gad, then up the latter stream for a short distance to its 
junction with the Seral gad, which tributary it follows uatil it finally reaches the 
Kasla Munda dbar at pillar No. 9, and continues along it southwards to pillar No. 12 
above the village of Duchinu. There is then a line of pillars ruaning east to pillar 
No. 17 above the village of Jagta ; the boundary then trends to the north-east above 
the villuge of Cheo aud meets the Dedra gad at pillar No. 25 just below the Garol gad, 
whence it proceeds up the former stream for about 30 chains to pillar No. 26. I¢ 
then leaves the stream and goes north up the hill, being marked by a line of pillars as 
fur as No. 34 when it trends south-east to pillar No. 39, and then again north to pillur 
No. 41. From this pillar it rans down a dhaér meeting the Koti gi at pillar No, 42, 
close to the Andri gad, it then ascends the former to a few chains above the latter géd 
at pillar No. 43, where it leaves the stream and runs east upa dhér to pillar 
No. 46. [From this point it desconds southwards by a line of pillars to the Batroli gad, 
and still going south runs up a tributary of this stream to pillar No. 51; stil continu- 
ing in a southerly direction above the village of Goksl and generally following ridges 
it meets the Innadi gad at pillar No. 57, and follows this stream for a short distance to 
pillar No. 58, whence it runs east up the Sdndidhér to pillar No. 59. It then turas 
south down a small ridge to pillar No. 61, where it meets a small stream, down which 
it runs westwards to the fire-line of the Kotigad forest, which it touches at pillar No. 62, 
a few chains above pillar No, 45 of the previously demarcated forest. From this 
point it takes a south-easterly course along the fire-line past the old pillar No. 46, 
down to pillar No. 63. Here it leaves the fire-line running east up a small ridge to 
the Mali dhar at pillar No. 64, whence it continues east by a line of pillars to the 
Jakar gid at pillar No. 74, and from this point it-asconds the stream for a short distance

along a dhar to the main: Chénsil ridge, which it follows back tu pillar No. 1. 


(3).— Nerdnu.

and 3, at pillar No. 3 it runs down a small stream to pillar No. 4 on 4 sheep- path,

it continues north up a db4r to pillar No. 7 whence it runs east along a line of pillars 
over very precipitous ground to pillar No. 11, and thence it goes north-west to pillar 
No. 12 on the main ridge. before mentioned, along which it returns to pillar No. 1. 

(4)— Naintwde, 

5, From pillar No. 1 on the ridge forming the watershed between the Rupin 
and the Supin, the boundary line descends. the Bali dhaér ina southerly direction to 
pillar No. 2, and then goes west in a straight line to-pillar No. 3-on the Kurbagéd. 
From this point it runs north-west up a small: stream, which it leaves at pillar No. 4, 
runping up a small dhér to pillar No. 5; thence it goes west along a line of 
pillars over very precipitous ground above the village of Koarbo to pillar No. 12 ona 
ridge above the Mairigdd. Form this point it goes down a small kbud to pillar 
No. 13 on the path to Kourbo at pillar No. 14, and thence goes west down a dh&r to 
the Muiri gad, which it meets at pillar No. 15. From this pillar it runs in a westerly 
direction up a dhar to pillar No. 16 and continues west in a straight line to pillar 
No. 17 in a small khud, up which it runs to the above mentioned watershed line, 
which it follows round to pillar No. 1. 


(5)—Ddimir. 


6. On the north thero is a demarcated line from a pillar on the main ridge 
between the Tons and the Supin to a point above the village of Guhg&ér, whence the 
boundary runs down the right bank of the river toa pillar about three-quarters of a 
mile below the Datmir bridge, and from the last-mentioned spot it runs up the Sun- 
drugéd back tothe main ridge. There is also a small detached block demarcated 
by ten pillars on the left bank of the Tons below Datmir village.

11. History. On the lst of May, 1864, all forests in Tehri-Garhwal were leased 
by His Highness the Raja for aterm of 20 years to the Biitish Government, who 
acquired the right with the approval of Llis Highness to demarcate and strictly 
conserve any of the dooddr forests in the state. This comprised deod&r tracts 
in the valleys of the Ganges, Jumna and Tons, the two latter being included in the 
Jaunsir Division, whieh paid for them a rental of Its. 4,500 per annum. The lease 
expired on tho 3ist of April, 1884, but was allowed by mutual agreement betwoen 
Flis Wighness the Raja and the Government to run on till the end of April, 1885. 
Iules for these forests, with the Raija’s consent, were issued in 1880, and the forests 
of Lambatach, Deota, Bamsu, Saris and Kotigad in the Tons valley and the forests 
of Shikara, Banél, Shalna, Deor4ni and Jaunpur (five small areas) in the Jumua valley 
were demarcated and protected from fire. In 1879-80 the leased forests of Tehri- 
Garhw4l and of Bashahr, in the valley of the Tons, Rupin and Pabar, together with 
the Khunigdd and Deogarh forests of Jaunsfr Biwar, were formed into a separate 
division called the Tons Division, but the two divisions were reunited as the Jaunsér 
Division in 1884. The terms of a new lease for 20 years from Ist April, 1885 (vide 
Appendix A) have been agreed to by the Raja, and the annual rental to be paid by the 


( 4 ) 


Jnunsir Division has been fixed at Rs. 9,000. The Réja is, however, unwilling to lease 
for the anme period abont 580 acres of chir forest below Bamsu, required for the

forests, but is prepared to grant any chir timber that Government may require for 
the purpose, without charge. 


12. The Lambataéch, Deota, Bamsn, Sards and Kotigadd forests were surveyed 
in 1880-82 on the scale of 4”== 1 mile by the Forest Survey Branch, and two admi- 
rable sheets are now available, in which every detail is most fuithfully depicted. 
For the remaining forests, which were demarcated in 1885, there are only rough sketch 
maps prepared by myself; but their survey by the Forest Survey Branch has been 
ordered by the Goveroment of India. 


CHAPTER II. 
DESCRIPTION OF THE Forests. 
(1) Deota. 


13, Area, boundaries, and blocks. —Tho total area of the forest is 4,937 acres. The 
outer boundary runs from the Kimari village to the Deota ridge, and is defined by a 
two-foot path. The forest has been diyided into ten blocks, bounded by natural 
features and permanent paths, and these are described in Appendix B. 


14. Configuration—The upper part of Deota may be regarded as consisting of 
four bays :— 
Jst—That inelnding the Kimféri block between the Kimi and Paténgni 
dhars, and drained by the Chabaigéd ; 


Qnd—Patdngni, between the Vaténgni and Kanga dhars, drained by the 
Kaéngagéd. 

3rd—Kanga and Jauraési between the ridges so named, and drained by 
the Misingad. 

4th—The Temple bay, round the head-waters of the Bagiar stream, and con- 
sisting of the remaining area between the Jaurdsi and Palia dhars, and 
comprising the Katatach, Dopta, Temple and Pirtil blocks. 


At their lower end, the four bays are rocky and precipitous and are here narrowed 
into steep rocky gorges and ravines, thus dividing the Deota forest into two clearly- 
defined portions, the upper part, described above, with deodér as the chief tree, 
and the Jower blocks, stocked with chir, called Bagifr and Tadiar, and situated on the 
right and left banks of the Bagiér stream. 


Slopes with an easy gradient below 30° are chiefly found in the central part of 
the Pfértil and Temple blocks, whilst on the west bank of the Buyiaér nadi there are 
only small areas with easy slopes, the largest in extent being found in the lower 
parts of the Kanga, Paténgui, and Kimari blocks. Elsewhere the ground is usually 
steep or precipitous with very short easier slopes botween the lines of steeper ground, 
which usually ron cast and west. The Dopta block, the upper part of the Katatach 
and Jaur&si, the Bagiér block and the lower part of the Kimari block, are especially 
precipituus and in many places are unworkable. The highest points in the forest 
are Lambatach peak, 10,469, and Rikshiss, 10,355 feet. The lowest point, the mouth 
of the Bagiargéd at its junction with the Tons, is about 3,409 feet. The general 
aspect of tho forest is southerly, but it varies extremely, for each block is divided by 
ravines and gullies, the opposite sides of which have different aspects. Tho southerly 
and south-eusterly aspects are usually the most ragged and precipitoas, whilat the 
ground facing uorth, which does not cover a large area, has genorally moderate or 
even gentle slopes. 

15, Rock and soil.—The rock is chiefly gneiss or mica-schist, with occasional 
blocks of quartzite, and is often exposed on precipitous ground. ‘The soil is superfi- 
cial or absent ou the steeper slopes, but elsewhere it is, as a rule, a light sandy loam, 


( 5) 


with a good laver of vegetable mould over most of the upper forests. “In most of the 
Partil and Temple blocks there is a deep sub-soil of red clay, a similar sub-soil also 
occurring in parts of the Kanga, Patdngni and Tadiar blocks. 


16, The stock.—Below the Lambatich and Dopta ridges there is a belt, chiefly 
stocked with karshu, stunted deodir, and a little morinda and rai; karshu being tho 
prevailing tree. This description of forest growth covers a considerablo area in Dopta, 
Janrdsi,and Kanga. The ground below the karshu belt and above the line of precipices 
is chiefly stocked with deodar, pure on the tnora precipitous parts as Dopta, Katatach 
5, Jaurdsi band 5, and highor part of Paténgni; elsewhere a-sociated with some 
spruce and silver fir, which predominate on the more gentle skopes of the Partil and 
Temple blocks, and on the slopes with a northerly agpeect in Kanga, Patdngni, and 
Jaurasi. The moru 9 oak is an important asseciate of deodar over a considerablo 
portion of the forest, and it is mest common on gentile slopes with deep soil, On the 
west side of the Bagidr nadi thero are some slepes with northerly and worth-casterly 
aspects: for example Patangni 3 and 4 with a large proportion of thie oak ; mort 
is also abundant east of the Bagiar, in the lower parts of the Temple, and the central 
and upper parts of the Partil block. 


Bin oak is common in the lower part of the deodir zone near the line of pre- 
cipices, the forest being usually ruther open and containing a misture in variable 
proportions of deodar, kail, ban, burdns, ayar, and occasional rai; the oak frequently 
predominating as in Partil 1 and 5 (upper part) and Pirtil 2 (lower portion), in 
the lower part of Temnp'e 5 and below the Kiméri road in the Kanga bloe.; whilst the 
upper part of the Kimiei block (compartments 1 and 2) mainly consists of the same 
ouk. This ork is to be met with at hizh elevations on warm southerly aspects, so that 
on the cold side of a given stream moru may abound, whilst at the same elevation on 
the other side there may be nothing but ban. This oak, together with bardns and 
ayar, rune also into the upper part of che chie zone, but is there most abundant iu 
shady ravines. 

A few walnut, maple, and horse chestnut are found associated with deodar near 
streams, and the birch is not uncommon. Blank slopes without any frost growth int 
the deodfr portion, are only found in the vicinity of the Deota ridge, in the lower part 
of the Partil bluck, and on precipitous slopes with a southern aspect elsewhore. Of thu 
undorgrowth little need be said ; ringal is found where the soil is deep and moist, and 
indigofera and desmodium are found on many of the blanks. 


Chir forms the forest in the lower part of all the blocks, np to the upper edgo of 
the line of precipices, genorally extending up to 7,000 feet, and the entire stock in the 
Bagide und Tadiar blocks is composed of this species, which also predominates in 
Paruil 6 and the lower part of Puitil 1, in Katatach 1, Jaurasi 1, Putaéngui 1 and 
5, and in Kimani 3.

17. Reproduction.—In the deodfr zone, reproduction is asa rule good, save 
whero the cover is too dense, deodar and kail scedlings being plentiful as well as rai 
and mora where these species aro found ; many of the open grassy slopes are getting 
rapidly filled np with kail seedlings and where there are seed-berrers with deodar 
as well, but seedlings are scarcer and sometimes absent in the indigofera serub. In 
some of the oak forest especially amongst the Lan, burdns, and ayir, there is often 
magnificent reproduction of deodar and kail; 2s, for example, in the Patangni block. 
The blocks which have been worked over deserve special notice In P4rtil, where the 
earliest cuttings were made, the natural reproduction of deodir is, on the whole, 
exceedingly satisfactory, there being a good deal of advanced growth and a large 
number of seedlings from the prolific sced-years of 1878 aud 1881. The only places 
not stocked naturally ina satisfactory manner are the moist ravines coming down 
from Leosita&ch and a number of grassy slopes with a south- westerly aspect. Jn the 
Temple block cuttings were made ata later date than in Partil, and although there is 
a large quantity of advanced growth, the number of seedlings is much less than in the 
latter block. 

In the Katatach block, which was worked over in 1881-83, reproduction is good 
for kail, deoddr aud chir wherever the cover is not tuo great. The lower road, which 
was used for export purposes, is now campletely choked with kal and deoddr 
seedlings. 

(2) Bamseu.

22. Reproduction.—In the mixed deodar and fir forest reproduction is slight, 
owing to the dense cover; what exists being mainly morinda with a little deodér on 
ridges. On grasay slopes and in other open places it is usually good, especially for 
kail, whilst deodaér seedlings are also pretty abundant, and even somo of the most 
exposed southerly slopos are gradually being filled up. Reproduction is, however, 
slight in Bameu 8 and only fair in the lower parts of that block. 


(3) Sarde,

Damraéi gid ineluded withia forest limits, and in addition a small area to the north 
forming a part of Damrdli 5. With the exception of Damraéli 3 and 5 the 
whole forest is on the southern bank of the gad. The slgpes are usually moderate or 
rather steep and broken by lines of precipitous ground, but precipitous broken 
ground is common above ravinos and over the greater part of Damréli 5 and 
Kanda 1, The aspect is gonerally north-casterly, but in Damrali 3 and 5_ it is 
routherly. The altitude alternates from 10,421 feet on the main ridge to 6,949 
fect at the place whero the firo-line crosses the Damréri gad. 


25. Rock and so:l.—A superficial loam with underlying clays, slate and mica 
schist prevails in the lower parts ; higher up on the portions facing north and east 
there is a deep moist loam on the less precipitous parts, whilst in Damidli 3 and 
most of Damrali 5 facing south the soil is superficial and sandy with quartzite rock 
often showing. 


26. Zhe atock.—In the upper part on northerly and easterly slopes there is a

main ridge. Deodar is most abundant on the precipitous ground and along ridges, 
Lowor down there is a mixture of rai, deodar and some kail, with a few broad-leaved 
species, ban, moru, burns, ayir, &c, the forest being very open in parts, with grassy 
slopes, especially in Kanda 1. The deodur are similar in quality to those in the 
Bainsu forest.

28. Area, boundaries, and blocks. —This forest, which has an area of 9,410 acres,

29. Configuration.—The whcele area forms the upper basin of the Kunigfd. 
The valley of this gad as ono ascends from the Tous is somewhat narrow, und there are 
no important tributaries ; but about a distance of six miles from the mouth of the stream 
it begins to spread out liko a fan, the pdéd giving off one imjortant tributary after 
another, and these again subdividing. Frou below, at the point where this begins, tho 
upper basin has the appearance of a large semi-circular bay bounded along the main 
ridge by, in many places, enormous and almost perpendicular masses of rock, tho rich 
deodadr forests being concealed im the valleys of the various tributaries, the principal 
being those which have given their names to the blocks enumerated nbove. The 
basin of those tributaries at their lower ends are narrowed into deep, rocky, | recipitous 
gorges, giving rise to some very fine waterfalls, ne, for example, one just below the 
camping-ground and another not far from the mouth of the Jodtirgad, This pecu- 
liarity helps to separate very distinotly the upper from the lower basin of the main 
gal even in a more marked manner than in the Deota forest, and, like it, the one is 
stocked with deodar and karshu, the other with chir. It is this line of precipices 
therefore, which mainly forms tho outer boundary of the forest. Tho whole of the 
forest faces south, with a succession of slopes on the right and left banks of the various 
gids, having, as a rule, north easterly or casterly and south-westerly or westerly aspects, 
respectively, This alternation of aspect exercises a most marked influence on the 
configuration of the ground, the exposed slopes being usually very precipitous and 
rocky and not unfrequently inaccessible, whilst on the others the gradient is generally 
moderate or steepish, not often exceeding °5°. 


30. Rock and soil.—The soil is fairly uniforw according to aspect and gradient ; 
on northerly slopes consisting of an excellent loam of a fuir depth, with a pood layer 
of humus, whilst on southerly ones it is either superficial or absent. In the upper 
part the rock is gneiss, in the lower part slate and quartzite, the latter being common 
along the line of precipices, as it isin the Deota forest ; and this geological featuro may 
be the cause of the suaden charge in gradient. 


31. Stoek —The stock in this forest exemplifies in an admirable manner the 
effect of aspect and altitude on forest vegetation in these regions ; thus along the main 
azidge there is the usual belt of karshu, rai, and morinda, whilst in the central part of 
tho forest, beginning from the south-west, we find a succession of ravines and streams, 
including the Kanga and Isteri gids, having a course almost due east, so that all tho 
slopes on their right Lanks face due north, and it is on these slopes, which have a 
moderate gradient, that we find dense forests of rai, morinda, moru, &c., with usually 
only a few scattered deodar, ckiefly on ridges; whilst on the opposite and precipitous 
plopes facing due south, we meet with either a pure deod4r forest or this species mixed 
with kail, or occasionally with a few rai; many of the slopes being, however, too 
precipitous for working purposes. Then come the Deotdl and Kunigdds running in a 
south-easterly direction, so that the elopes on either bank have, respectively, uorth- 
easterly and sonth-westerly aspects; on the former slopes we no longer find the dense 
fir forests charucieristic uf the Kaéngagad, but a mixed forest of deodar, rai, and moru, 
and in the lower parts either kail and deoddfr or ban and oak, with large well-grown 
deodar, rai, and kai]; on the south-westerly slopes the deodar is alinost pure, but there 
ure only a few workable places. Neat we come to the DwA&nti, Sili and Jodi: gads, all 
running almost due south, and consequently, with the compartments formed out of their 
basins, facing duo east and west ; in compartments with an easterly aspect in the Dwanti 
and Silig4ds there is, for the most part, a splendid deodar forest with a few rai, moru, 
and kail, b4n and kail predominating in tke lower parts with scattered deodar, whilst on 
similar slopes of the Jodérgad there is a pure deodér forest above, with open grassy 
slopes containing scattered deodar and kail lower duwo. On the westerly sloj'’cs there 


( 9 ) 


is almost a pure deodar forest and the ground is. mostly workable, but in the Jodér 

g4d block moru oak is common at this aspect and occasionally predominates, although 

there is always a considerablo proportion of deo lfr. Thus as the aspect voers round 

from due north to duo east, so does the stock change gradually from a pure fir to a 

pure deodér forest ; which latter type is almost always found on parts facing. the west 

and the south, but is here often unworkable owing to the precipitous character of the 

ground. ‘There is often a fair proportion of moru in tha denser forest, and ban oak 

ussuciuted with kail and an occasional deodir, occupies a considerable area in the 
luwer portions of the forest, as for example in Moriru 3 and 4 and in Kunig&d 1; such 
forests are admirably suited for the growth of deodér. Broad-leaved trees are usually

Ono northerly and north-casterly elopes the ground is woil covered with numerous

deodar are branchy and badly-grown, many of the finest trees having been worked out

(8) Lambatéch. 


45. Area, boundaries and blocks. —The total area of the forest is 2,883 acres. The 
outer boundary which runs from L»mbatach peak round to Kiméri village, is defined 
by a two-foot path, the castern boundary being formed by the Lambat&ch ridge which 
separates the Lambuatdch and Peotn forests, Kandaér isa small detached block south- 
west of Karoli village. Tho rectification of the boundary near Thanwira village has 
now heen completed. 


Tho forest has been divided into ten blocks, separated by natura) features and 
pormencnt paths which are described in appendix B. 


46. Configuration.—Lambatich may be generally described as consisting of one 
large bay, which occupies the centre of the forest, being bounded on the north by the 
Ghoria and Jogi ridges, and on the sonth by the Chakora and Samani ridges; and of 
three smaller bays, riz, Thanwara on the north, and Loh&su and Inadi on the south. 
The central bay is drained by numerous feeders, which join and form the Butarnu gad, 
Asa rule, the slopes on the right bank have a southerly and those on the left bank a 
northerly aspect; and a siuular variety in the aspect of the slopes is observable in the 
smaller bays. Throughout the forest, rocky precipices and steep slopes alternate in 
a very irregular manner with slopes of a more casy gradient, but moderate slopes are 
commoner than in the Deota forest, and form the greater part of the area in the 
Chakora, Garasna, Banarpani, and Thanwara blocks facing west, whilst Inadi, Naini- 
tach and Ghoria facing south and south-west are rocky and precipitoiws: The high- 
est point is Lambatdch peak, which has an elevation of 10,538 foet ; the lowost block, 
Kandar, is about 6,800 feet above sea level. 


47. Ruek and soil.—The rock is principally shale and clay slate, but gneiss is 
found on the Lambatéch peak. ‘he soil is rich, and in many parts consists of a dvop 
loam, with an abundance of vegetable mould in the valleys and ravines and on the 
better stocked slopes 

48. The etock.—Along the Lambhatach ridge there is a narrow belt of karshu oak 
which is of greatest width and nearly pure below the Lambatfch peak. Lower down 
along the ridge it gradually narrows and becomes mixed with morinda and deodfc. 
Below this belt of karshu, and eatending downwards along tho valleys and ravines, 
the forest is deodar, mixed with rai and morinda, and a few kail. At high elevations 
on westerly aspects where tho slopes are moderate, as in Banérpéni and part of Chakora, 
there was a dense forest of rai and morinda with a proportion of I and II class deodér 
everywhere up to the ridge, but this has been opened out by the ringing of a very 
considerable number of the firs, with a view to encourage the reproduction of deodar. 
On slopes with a southerly aspect, especially the more precipitous and rocky ones, 
thore is either a pure deod&r forest,as in the upper parts of tho Inadi, Ghoria and 
Lobasu blocks, or extensive grassy blanks, which is the condition of nearly the whole of 
Dhamti, such blanks occurring indeod in all the blocks save Bandrpani ; elsewhere in the 
centre of tho forcst there is usually an open mixed forest of deodar, kail and moru or 
ban, the deodér consisting only of some old scattered trees, indved III and 1V class 
trees are usually rare, Leing only numerous in the Nainit4ch and Ghoria blocks, 
This scarcity of the younger classes shows that the forest must have been steadily de- 
teriorating for a great nuinber of years before its total destruction was checked by the 
Forest Department. The ouks, moru and bin, are mainly found in the Garasna block. 
In the lowest parts of the forest with westerly aspect in the Thanwdra, Lohasu, Chakora 
blocks and also inthe Kanddér block deodf&r is mixed with a considerable number of 
kail. The 1 class deodar having grown upio an open forest, especially on the more 


( 12 ) 


unfavorable aspects, are branchy and badly grown, bunt most of the best trees were out

desmodium, and is most dense on damp northerly slopes, where it is mixed with bal-

49, Reproduction.— As the whole of this forest has been worked out, it is import- 
ant to note its presont condition as ragards repro luction, Writing in 1878, Mr. Greig 
says— There are few more enjoyable sights than the open slopes of Lambat&ch, 
formerly seas of grass or clearly nibbled or burnt hill-sides, now evenly covered with 
deodfr and kail seedlings ; it was certainly most gratifying to observe the immense 
improvement our treatment of them has effected ; and not only on grassy slopes, but 
throughout the forests.” Mr. Greig must have been referring particularly to most of 
the slopes with a westerly aspect. 


The pregent edndition may be stated thus. In the upper parts of the forest the 
reproduction is usually fair, there being gonerally a number of deod&r seedlings, distri- 
buted throughout the different blocks but most abundant on the ridges. In Banér- 
pani and Gaifsna where the girdling operations have been carried out, the soil has as a 
result, become completely stocked with a dense growth of soft wooded species, such as 
willows, viburnums, honoysucklea, maples, &., but advance growth of deodér is een 
springing up here and there amidst this tangled mass. On exposed slopes reproduce 
tion is unsitisfactors, and ouly a very few seedlings of kail and deoddr ean ho fonrd 
in the In4di and Dhdémti blocks, or on the exposed grassy slopes in the Nainitach 
and Ghoria blocks. In other placer covered with brushwood consisting of indigofera 
and desmodium there are also very few seedlings, which may be due to the heavy and 
low cover of these shrub: during and after the rains. In parts at a moderate elevation 
down to the outer boundary of the forest on slopes with a westerly or north-westerly 
aspect, as in Luhdsu, Chahora, Gardsna and Thanwara, there is magnificent advanced 
growth of kail forming dense thickets, with young deodar duing the same near its seed- 
bearers, whilst almost everywhere some are to be found coming up amongst the kail, 
but near*the Lodigdr dhar in the Lohésu block, the reproduction is almost entirely of 
kail. These young kuil are generally trom 6 to 17 years old, having all sprung up since 
the forests were protected from fire and they are from 10 to 80 teet high. The annual 
shoots of this tree are very long, in some places exceeding two feet, in fact some were 
measured with a length of three feet. 


Kotigdd. 

50. Area, boundaries and blocks. —The area of the forest is 5,076 acres, which is in- 
clusive of the newly-demarcated Gokal block of 1,108 acres. The outer boundary rung

A small camping ground has also been marked off with four pillars near Gokal 
village, and this includes a field three chains by one chain belonging to Gopi Chand 
of Jaituri, to whom compensation will have to be paid. 


51. Confiyu:ation.—The forest is formed of slopes on the left bank of the Koti- 


gid frum the Lambatich forest to the Bannil: gad, and of all that portion of the basin 
of the Baunali gad comprised within the forest boundary. 


Amongst the minor basins the most important are those of the Muoldi gad, tho 
two Murali gids, the Raitu gid and the Inédi gad, all these streains running in a 
north-westerly direction save the Indi gid which flows towards the south west. With 


( 18 |) 


the exception of the Gokal block which faces south with minor north-westerly and 
south-easterly aspecta, the forest faces north by west with minor easterly and west- 
erlyazpects on the right and left banks of the various tributaries of the Banndli an! 
Koti gids. Thore is a very large extent of moderate even slopes in this foreat vury-

various tributaries with enstoily aspect, except that the ground is precipitous immo-

right banks and there is a considerable area of this character in the upper part of the 
Gokal block. The highest pont is Lambatich peak 10,538 feet, the lowest is 6,814 
fect on the fire-line at pillar No. 34. 


$2. Rock and soil.—On moderate slopes there is a fairly deep moist loam with 
good leaf mould in parts and often with a large pereentage of clay Tho soil is super- 
ficial on the steeper portions, with frequent out crops of rock, which is usually gneiss, 
clay-slato or quartzite. 


53. The stock.—Along the main ridge there is a dense forest of rai, morinda 
and karshu, including Gokal 4, neurly the whole of Balcha, Mardli 6 and Ola 7; 
lower down on favorable aspects there is a mixture of rai, morin Ja, deodar and kail, the 
two former being abundant at the higher elevations, the kail at the lower, but deodar 
very seldom predominates On the steeper slopes in the Gokal block deodar predo- 
minates mixed with a few rai and kail and in the lower parts with ban, whilst grassy 
slopes are not uacommon. Elsewhere on westerly slopes there is either an open furest 
of scattered deodar, with soine rai, kail and bin, or ban with a few kail only, or simply 
ferns or grass. Immediately above the fire-line in Murali 5andin Ola 5 and 6, there 
are extensive open grassy fern clad moderately oven slopes facing north and north-west, 
containing some old and scattered kail, besides ona or two deodar, rai and morn. 


Tho deoddér growing as it does on the more precipitous and open parts of the 
forest ia usually rather branchy and not well grown, but there are some vory fine trees 
in places, especially in Balcha and in Gokal 2, where one or two trees were measured 
with a girth of 25 feet.

54, Reproduction.—There are very few seedlings in the upper fir forests awing to

CHANSIL. 


55. Area, boundaries and blocks,—The area of the forost is estimated at 11,805

the Chansil ridge. It has been sub-divided into soven blocks, viz., Duchanu, Jagta, 
Dedragad, Andrigéd, Boktiragad, Tara Mundal and Maunda. 


56. Conjiguration.—Chansil comprises the whole of the forests in the upper 
basin of the Koti géd,ie¢., beyond its junction with the Bannali géd. In this area 
ere included the busin of three large tributaries all falling into the main stream from

59. Reproduction.—This ia generally unsatisfactory, morinda seedlings only

60. Summary.—Thus we see that in these forests there are large areas of preci- 
pitous ground, especially on southerly aspects, and that in some forests much of this 
ground is unworkable especially in the Chansil, S4hlra, Nordnu and Ddtmir forests. 
On the more favorable aspects there are howevor large areas of moderate evenly slop- 
ing ground, espocially in the Lambat&ch, Kotigad, Séhlra and Naintw &r forests. Along 
the upper portion of all the forests there is a belt of variable width of karshu and the 
firs; lower down on southerly slopes and on nearly ull precipitous ground thore is an 
open forest of pure deodér, whilst on the cooler slopes with steepish gradients there is

of the Tehri forests owing to their situation nearer the snow ; thus from observations 
which have been made at Deota it would appear that the avorage rainfall is not much 
over 40 inches as compared with 60 at Chakrata.

64. Fellings.—Departmental timber operations were begun in these forests in 
October, 1869, but on account of the oxtreme cold and snow vor y little was done 
during tho financial year of 1869-70. Colonel Pearson in the annual rerort for that 
year says.— The work in the Tons has been commenced uader great difficulties from 
the daily increasing scarcity of labor in the hills, and the difficulty of keeping up a 


( 16 ) 


rogular supply of food for large gangs of workmen in a country os yet unprovided 
with roads. The bites of the * potu fy ”’ also are 2 terrible scourge to plainsmen, and 
just before my return to the Tons last June, 180 pair of sawyers all bolted uff, doclar- 
ing that nothing should induce thom to work in such a place. This has necessitated 
the collection of hill-sawyers, whose work is of much inferior quality to that of the 
plainsmen and in the meantime sawing is nearly stopped.” However, by perseverance 
and judicious management the prejudices of the sawyers were eventually overcome 
and work goes on steadily now without any mishaps. 


Tho first forests to be worked were Datmir and Lambat&ch, and from the latter 
forest the last timber was exported in 1875, In March, 1876, the Deota forest was 
tapped and ever since that date work has been carried on there in a proper and sys- 
tematic way, compartment by compartment. The outturn from 1869-1885, when the 
old lease expired was about 6,50,000 deodar sleepers, about three-fourths being metre 


gauge.

so that 350 acres are protected for every mile of fire-line. Interior fire-lines were also

Some portions of the forest, especially those above 8,000 fect, and all the dense 
fir forests, are difficult to burn and world often escape without fite-lines; but it is 
impossible to separate them from the regular deodar forests, and such damp localities 
help to check the spread of a fire, rendering protection much easier ; so thut fires 
when thoy do occur in the deodar forests are never very extensive. 


That protection” is, however, essential to the maintenance of the forests, if they 
gre to be worked systematicully, I venture to assert that no one who has visited those 
blocks which have been protected for many years will deny. The westerly slopes of 
Lambat&ch aro particularly favorable examples of the wonderful :esults of such protec- 
tion (vide paragraph, 50.)

75. Cleanings of kail over deodér.—In_ those portions of the Lambatich forost 
where deodar is associated with kail and on many of the blank grassy slopes, an 
abundant growth of the latter species has sprung up, since the protectich: of the 
forest from cattle and fires. The seed of this pine is light, and has large 
wing, and is therefore carried by the wind to great distances, whilst ‘it requires 
fio shelter and springs up readily on open blank spaces. In the Thanwfra, Lohfsy,, 
Garisna and Chakora blocks there are extensive thickets of this pine from 10. 
to 30 feet bigh with usnally a fair number of deodfr seedlings underneath’ 
them. Owing to the extremely rapid growth of kail in its youth it was at first feared 
that nearly all the young doodar would be choked by its more rubust companions, a 
pure forest of kail being the result. In 1881, therefore, over 36 acres in Chakora nnd 
over four acres in Garasna, where it was feared that the young dcodar were being chok- 
ed, the smaller kail trees were entirely cut away, while the larger trees were ringed. 
‘this operation was continued the following year over an additional 75 acres, but it was 
found that the exposed deoda&r, rendered delicate hy the cover of the kail, becamo yellow 
and were bent down by snow, and it was ordored in future that the kail should only 


( 20 ) 


be rentoved in place§ where the deodar was really suff ring from its cover, and that, as 
a rule, only the lower branches of the kail were to be lopped off. It was considered 
that a certain proportion of kail was extremely useful as affording lat-ral protection to 
the young deodar, which frequently lose their vigorous appearanve and become yellow 
if suddenly exposed ; whereas isolated deodar which has grown always in the open is not 
so liable to this delicate appearance provided the drainage is sufficient. Up tu date

At first tho system adopted wasin Limbat&ch to stiip off a belt of bark 14 feot to two 
feet broad, whila in Deota a ring six inches broad and two inches deep was cut into 
the wood. In both cases the trees, asa rule, died during the second year, but the first- 
mentioned system being the cheaper of the two is now only employed. In the earlier 
operations in Lambatach too great a proportion of the spruce and silver tir were ringed, 

the result of the admission of so much light on the rnch and moist soil being a loxuriang 
growth of sbrubs and herbs which have almost completely prevented deodér seedlings 
from springing up. Benefiting by the experience thus gained, in the Temple block only 
such rai and morinda as were interfering with the growth of the neighbouring deodar 
were kitted, and groat care was taken not to lay the ground bare. Finally it was conside- 
red | bast to ring the trees a considerable timo before the selection fellings of deod4r were 
mate, in order to throw light upon the ground, so that when u favorable sved-yaer 
oceurred the place might be speedily covered with seedlings of deoddr, and so the 
ground would be well stocked with advanced growth before cutting out the mature 
deodar, but in order to avoid the danger of a crop of brambles and other shrubs by 
the admission of too much light, it was considered prudent to romove the inferior 
species very gradually and to go over the same ground two or three times at intervals 
of several years. Thus these operations in the Kanga and Patdngni blocks Have 
preceded the fellings, It is also now intended at the time when the trees are ringed 
to remove ull low bushes, &c , and to roughly prepare the ground under sved-bearing 
deoduar. 


I: bas also been found cheapest to do the ringing in April and May when the bark 
ean be readily removed; thus in 1881-82 when the work was done at this season the 
cost was one and a half pie per tree only, whereas in the following year when it was 
done at anvther time the cost per tree was over one annua. In addition to the spruce

and silver fir other inferior species such as moru, bin, karshu, burdns, ay4r and kail 
are now also ringed.

linca in the neighbourhood of seod-beuring trees and subsequently found the lines fairly 
well covered with seedlings to a distance of about 50 yards from the parent trees. Some 
pntches have also been cleared in the inaigofera and desmodium scrub in order to 
encourage deod4ér reproduction, and the results linvoe been fairly sneecessful ; bat in all 
cases where the svil is prepared it will be better to sow up artificially instead of 
awaiting nutural reproduction, as the cust of sowing, ouce the ground is prepared, is 
a mere trifle,

81. The Chakrita-Tons road is from six to eight feet broad and is passable for 
ponies and pack-animals, It was begun in 1870, when 35 miles were constructed from 
Deoban to Maindrot, and thence along the left bank of the Tons to Tiuni bridge, the 
line being excellently chosen and the work well and cheaply executed at a cost of 
about Rs 360 per mile In 1876-77 it was carried on to the Tadiar bridge, a distance 
of four miles for less than Ks. 1,000, and from the bridge the road was continued right 
up the hill to Deota, a further distance of 6} miles at a cost of a little over Rs. 3,500, 
this part of the road having to be carried throagh some very precipitous ground where 
the blasting work was very heavy. The road is an exceedingly useful one, for, besides 
opening out the Jaunsér forests, it connects tho Tons sleeper works with the plains, 
aud is the road along which the whole food supply for these works is brought from 
Chakréta. It is the main road through the upper portion of Jaunsér and a grant in 
aid of its maintenance is now made by the district board.

A wire-rope suspension bridge at Tiuni was built in 1870-71 by an officer of the

been completed very satisfactorily at a cost of Rs. 2,864. Itis the steadiest bridye 
of its kind 1 have ever crossed ; the span is 146 feet and the road-way six feet broad. 
All grains, stores, &c , for the Deota works cross the Tons by this bridge.”” In 1878-79, 
it was repaired at a costof HRs. 503, 


At Datmir a suspension bridge was built in 1871-72 for Rs. 700, and it was re- 
paired in 1885-86 for Ry. 93. In thesame year at Naintwar trees were given and the 
inhabitants put up a good, firm, wooden bridge under tho superintendence of the forest 
officer, and this was repaired in 1834-85. In ths year the old pahdri bridge at 
Arakot was completety reconstructed at a cost of Ks. 572 on the old hill system. The 
span is 87 feet. The piers are constructed of rubblo masonry tied together with wood. 
Into these are fixed three Inyers of beams slightly inclined upwards, each layer rather 
longer than the one immediately under it ; the layers are supported on each other and 
the uppermost layers are the main beams of the bridge. The bridge above Chco is a 
capital wooden stracture built by the timber contractor working in tho Andiigdd in 


1881-85.

86. The only demand which can at present be met from (hese forests is for

and the spruce and silver fir have proved excellent for the purpose, but the cost of 
transport at present prohibits this market being opened out. Of late also there have 
been enquities mado regarding the cost of the delivery of these woods for paper 
making purposes, and this may in the future give rise to a very important industry. 
Some years ago it was proposed to establish a factory for the impregnation of pine 
sleepers from the north-west Himélaya, and in 1878 Sie D. Brandisand Dr. Warth 
wrote memoranda on the question, but the project has not been carried out. The 
Mannger of the North-West State Railway, however, issued a circular last year stating 
that he wished all his sleepers to bo prepared in India in future, and that he was 
willing to buy firs and pines as well vs deodar, To supply the sleeper market, over six 
Jakhs of sleepers, of which about three-fourths were metre gauge, have been exported 
from the working circle under the old lease. The broad gauge sleepers were sold to 
the Agra and Scindia Railway, but now only metre gange sleepers are exported, and 
these are all taken by the RAjputana-Malwa Railway now amalgamated with the 
Bombay and Baroda lines: a few broad gauge sleepers were also disposed of to the 
Fxecutive Engineer, Muttra Bridge, and to the Gun Carriage Factory, Fatehgarh, 
whilst some metre gauge sleepors were takon by the Muttra Provincial Light Railway,

The export works made under the old Jease will now be briefly described. 


90 Lambatdéch —In this forest a tramway and a slide wore constructed for 
export purposes, and a report on them was made by Mr. Bagshawe in 1873 from 
which the following inforrantion is tuken. 


The average distance from the centro of the deod4r zone to the Pabar, the nearest 
river available for water carriage is about five miles. A two-foot gauge tramway mado 
of wooden rails and sleepers 5,188 feet long was laid down below the Chakora block 
along the hillside, the line twisting round sinall ridges, and again running across sinall 
plateaux. Asarule, the cuttings were small and mainly throngh oarth and the line 
was nearly level The rails of the tramway were keyed into the sleepers with a 
wooden key which unswered well; it held the rails firmly, was casy of removal and 
very much cheaper thau spikes or screws, When the tramway was first made, the use 
of four-wheeled trucks was proposed like those used at Mandhol, but experiment 
showed that on a line with 80 many sharp curves on it, this mode of carriage would be 
both slow und expensive. The two-wheeled truck (see drawing No 1,) capable of 
carrying twelve sleepers at a journey was therefore devised by Mi. Greig asa sub- 
stitute, and unswered admirably throughout. As it was found that whea the body of 
the truck was fixed on the axles and the axles fixed on the wheels, there wus no play 
whatever and the trucks would not go round the curves, this was remedied by muking 
the wheels revolve on the axle instead of being fixed to it, whilst the carriage moved 
on the aale on an irun pivot. This truck required two men to work it. 


The price paid for running thesleepers along the tramway was at fist 4 pie and 
later on 3 pie per sleeper, 5 per cent. on the total price being paid to the head man 
of the gang employed. In all 92,963 sleepers were thus carried by abont 25 men in 
atout seven months. ‘Tho tramway cost Re. 8,150 and by its use a saving of Rs. 1,400 
was effected. 


91, The slide was a dry one (+ ¢., the moving power was the momentum given 
to the sleeper by the fall in the gradient of the slide, not water-power), It was 
formed of three kurries 13’ % 12” X57” wedged in block sleepers (short logs) with a 
nick cut in them to form a bed for the kurries, a small qnantity of water was intro luced 
at intervals to remove the danger of fire from friction between the sleepers and the bed 
of the slide, The length of the slide was 1 mile and 1,052 feet, and the total fall wag 
2,687 fect. When the line was first selected, it was ut once foreseen that two groat 
difficulties had to be overcome, (1) to prevent the sleepers leaving the bed of the slido— 
(2) to check their velocity while in transit. The depth of the slide was at first seyen 


( 2 ) 


inches, and it was thought that the use of brakes placed at intervals would « vercoma 
the difficulty. Trial brakes were at first put up with light one-inch planks hanging 
down, at intervals of 100 feet, but no successful results were attaine|!. The distance 
between tho trial brakes was then shortened, and two and three-inch planks used, but 
still the velocity of the sleepers remained unchecked ; finally brakes were tried 15 feet. 
apart, but even this was of no avail It was then resolved to divide the slide into 
two sections, cnt down the depth to 54 inches and cover it throughout by fixing 
planks to the top. This covered-in slide proved a great success, although it had 
to be worked carefully. The sleepers wont down the slide about three per minute, 
but Mr. Greig thinks, if necessary, they might have been sent dewn at the rate 
of ten per minuie. At the bottom of tho slide a pool was formed by damming up a 
breach of the Paébar river ; this pool was about five feet deep, all the sleepers came 
into it and were carried out of it down the waste-water channel to a line of boulder 
stones where they were taken out, stacked and mide over to tho floating contractor. 
The number of sleepers damaged in transit wag from 2 to 3 per cent. The &lide cost 
nearly Its. 11,000, and it is estimated that the saving effected by its use was nearly Rs. 
10,000. The cost of sending sleepers down the slide was about Re. 1-8 per cent. 
Tho Jower half of the slide was burnt in 1873 and reconstructed by removing a portion 
of the uj:per section which was not replaced, 


92. Altogether 158,224 sleepers were xawn up in Lambat&ch, of these 38,142 
sleepors were in Thanwara, the most northern part of the forest; these sleopers were 
collected in a depdt at the top of the final descent and thence carried on men’s 
backs down to the river @ 2 annas 6 pies each. ‘Tho romainder of the sleepers all 


went down the slide. 


93. Deota.—Tho export works which’ have been constructed in this forest and 
which are atill in use have been described by Mr. Moir in his report on the Deota 
sledge road, dated 5th September, 1885, which may be briefly snmmazised here. The 
two principal export works are the slide and the sledge road. 


The slide, which is 12,192 feet in length, was constructed during 1870-78 at a cost 
of Rs. 26,000, whilst up to the end of March 1885 Ks. 7,988 have been spent on repairs. 
The gradient varies from 5 to 22 degrees, the best gradient being about 15 degrees, 
and the total fall is 1,300 feet. It consists of a trough composed of three beams, 
12’ x13" x5” roughly jointed and firmly wedged into block sleepers. Those beams 
which are of ch{r, have been found only to last three or four years at the longest, so 
that the entire slide has had to be reconstructed more than once. It would have 
been much cheaper in the end to have made it of deodar, as this wood Jasts indefinitely 
in the hills, whilst chir rots in two or threo yoars when exposed to alternations of heat 
and moisture. The inside measurement of the slide is 13 inches wide by 8 inches high 
as it was originally constructed for the sliding of broad gaugo sleepers ; for metre gauge 
sleepers a lighter slide measuring 67 37 x83” X43” would suffice. There are in all 
eight main bridges varying from 30 to 70 feet in length, as well as several igavy rock . 
cuttings and retaining walls 20 to 25 feet high The slide is worked hy means of 
a good flow of water, which is let in from the Pagiar nala by means of troughs situated’ 
about a qaarter of a mile apart, very little water being required when the cradient is of 
22 degrees, but a plentiful supply of water being absolutely necessary when it is less 
than 18 or 20 degrees. The slide is principally worked during the rains, as there is not 
sufficient water available at other seasons, except during February, after the melting 
of the snow. With a good supply of water a sleeper takes about ten minutes travell- 
ing down the slide, and 1,200 sleepers can be slid in one working day of 10 or 12 
hours. On arrival at the Tadiir depot on the tanks of the Tons, the sleepers are 
thrown out of the slide by means of a kind of tablo provided with a slanting benm 
agaist which they strike and they are then stacked. After the monsoon or during 
February the sleepers are slid directly into the Tons, but this cannot be done during 
the floods, Since its completion to the end of March, 18%5, 407,800 sleepers, of 

T 


( 26 ) 


which 135,000 were brond gauge have been exported by its aid and the saving this 
effected has been ostimated at Rs. 15,014. 


94 The sledge road is the first of its kind in India, and was oncetructed in 
1883-84 principally under the direct supervision of Mr, Hfobart-Hampden, at a cost 
Rs. 8,587, including ten sledges. The total length of the road is 6,877 feet, includiug 20 
bridges aggregating 1,068 feet in length. The amount of hard rock-catting requising 
blasting was about 14,000 cubic feot, and thero are nearly 7,500 cubic feet of refain- 
ing walls, The gradient varies from 5 to 11 degiecs, the average being 8 degrees, 
and experience has shown that the bost gra dient is from seven to eight degrees Some 
of the curves are extremely sharp, having a radius of 20 to 22 feet only, The fall in 
the total length of rvad is 835 fect. 


The construction is as follows: —Two lines of metre gauge sleepers (from tejoctions 
in the forest) were laid down longitudinally 2 feet 9 inches apart, the ends being jointed 
and firmly fixed by means of moru pegs; across these, at distances of two feet six 
inches apart the transverse sleepers aro placed and these are either jointed or peggoad 
to the Jower sleepers by means of morn pegs, but on bridges and sharp curves, 6 inch 
nails are used. Across the upper sleepers aro cut the groovesin which tho sledges run; 
they are situated two feet apart, and have breadths varying from four to six inches, 
according to the curves, their depth being from balf to three-quarters of an inch. The 
centralportion of the roadway between the longitudinal sleepers is ballasted up toa level 
with the transverse sleepers, which prevents any shifting. The’ sledges in use are 4 
feet long, two feet seven inches wide (outside measurement) and threo feet nine inches 
high and consist of two strong wooden runners placed two feet two inches apart into which 
the uprights forming the front part of the sledge are firmly fixed. The front uprights 
against which the sluepors rest are bolted together by means of two or three light 
bolts, and ail the joints ure firmly secured by means of iron plates and pivots, 


The runners as they get worn down have soles attached; these soles and the 
runners are made of moru, wlulst the rest of the sledgo is made of sh shan. A 
sledge requires two men to work itand it is brought back to the upper depdt on 
one of the men’s shoulders. The number of sleepers carried per sledge per day 
varies from 100 to 12.) and the rate paid is 2-4 pies per sleeper compared with one 
anna paid formerly for carriage on coolies’ backs. Itis estimated that a saving of 
Rs. 5,600 will be effected by the uce of this read besides great saving in labor 
which is difficult to secure at all seasons. There is a break of very precipitous 
ground between the end of the sledge rond and tho slide, the drop being about 2,000 
feet and sleepers are carried down this place to the head of the slide by coolies, 


A dry slide on a similar principle to that at Lambatach has now been made for 
1,452 feet, bur the gradients are greater, attaining over 40 degrees in some places. 
Hence the use of breaks iv ybsolutely necessary to regulate the pace of tho sleepers and 
these are supplied by clappers (planks) heavier at the lower end and working on iron 
a:les. 


The section »nside the slide measured 9” x 74” so a8 to accommodate broad gauge 
sleepers, as there has always been an uncertainty about the demand for metre gauge 
slecpers. Jt would have been better for tho latter if the section had teen reduced to 
&” x54” as the sleepers now occasionally turn on their edges and jam against the 
covering 


Slides cf this character should be made as straight as possible and of uniform 
etadient, to prevent sleepers ploughing into the bottom. 


The sleepers come down the slide in 35 seconds, but owin z to hitches occurring- 
not more than 50 per hour can be passed down, 


It is no longer used, owing to the difficulty of stopping the sleepers at the lower 
end and to the fact that many of them were considerably damaged in transit.

The export lines from the other forest will be discussed in chapter VII. 

97, These all converge to the Tons, down which all our sleepers will have to be

‘Détmir and.the junction of tho Tons and Pinchganga. There is a fall of nearly 2,500 
feet from Ditmir to Naintwér, or above 100 feet per mile ; and after passing the Panch- 

ganga ‘there should be no great difficulty in bringing down any number of sleepera 
from.that forest during the rainy season.

Timber is usually launched about the 15th of October at Tadid4r and elsewhere 
onthe Tons, about the 15th of September on the Pibar, and in the Kotig&d on the 
15th of August. Owing to the nature of the river, sleepers and other scantling can be 
only floated separately until Dakhpatthar is reached. The men employed on this work 
are professional mullahs from the two small hill states of Mandi and Bildspnr in the 
Panj&b. The mullahs are divided into two classes, Sernaiwalas, who use inflated goats’ 
or cows’ skins, and “ Tarus.”? Food is willingly supplied to the men by the neighbouring 
villagers, and each workman receives Re. 1 atthe end of the week to make the necessary 
purchases, their accounts being settled on the arrival of the sleepers at Dikhpatthar. 
This arrangement is found to be much preferable to cmploying a bania for the 
food-supply.

The most troublesome places onthe river are the Katin, Khilona, Kilaur, and 
other ‘dandas’ or rocky places, and the I[atur, Koti, Anbitta and other ‘ ghooms’ or 
backwators. The Katén dunda is the most difficalt of all, boing a narrow rocky gorge 
over a milo long, and if the river is low it may take a month before all the sleepers 
are got past it. It could probably be greatly improved by tho use of dynamite.

the small pieces.

To the cost of floating departmentally should be added half anna per sleeper for

101. The Dakhpatthar boom.—This boom, intended to catch ull timber fluated 
down the Jumna and the Tons, was first put up by Mr. Winton, but it was taken over 
by the Forest Department in the raine of 1881. The boom as at first constracted was 
not strong enough to resist unusually high floods, and the upper face being ragged, 
timber striking against it stuck and then got sucked in underneath instead of sliding | 
along and passing at once into the side bay formed by the line ‘‘dauri’? The boom 
has now been reconstructed on an improved principle and the present system is 
shown in the accompanying plate, vide drawing No.4. The longitudinal pieces are 
fixed by means of pieces of wire-rope passing through, as bolts would be apt tu break 
in the case of sudden strain being brought against the boom and a certain amount 
of flexibility has to be allowed. It cost Rs 1,150 including Rs. 500 for hawsers and 
wire-ropes, which will last many voars., The boom is hept up during the cold and hot 
weather, and removed just before the raius during which season a gang of mullahs 
is maintained, 


102. Catching tunber during the floods —During the floods of 1884 and especially 
after the great flood of 1st October, 1834, upwards of 41,000 merchants’ sleepers 
were secured by this department, the total number which wout adrift being about 
50,000. This great flood rose to within one fvot of the floor of the forest chauhi 
at Dakhpatthar and took place at a most unusual season of the year. Some sleepers , 
came from Tadiaér in one day, and some wero carried as far as Delhi. 


103. Scourage.—During the transit of the sleepers from Tadifr to Dakhpatthar 
they are subjected to a certain amount of wear and tear against rocks and so forth. 
To land a slecper 6’ X 8” x 44 it is quito sufficient to cut it 6)’ x 83” 42” in the foreat, 


104, floating to Delhi.—After the timber is caught at the boom it is bound into~ 
rafts consisting of 120 broad gauge or 160 metre gauge sleepors The sizeof the rafts are 
regulated principally by the Western Jumna Canal rules, (Appendix H), which’ 
stipulate that no raft is to be more than 5U-feet in Iongth. As a rule, the timber is 
only rafted down the canal as fur as Dadupur, where it is taken throngh an escape 
into the main Jumna to Réyyhat. In the case of the canal being closed for repairs, tho 


( 29 ) 


rafts are taken over the weir and floated straight to Rijgh&t. At this place the rafts 
are broken up and bound into large rafts containing about 600 sleepers and proceed 
to Delhi. 

The canal dues are high, being about Re. 0-1-1 from Bogriwala to Delhi, but 
only 5-6 pies from Bogriwala to Dadupur hence every eftort is made to take the timber 
down the main Jumna when there is sufficient water. The Eastern Jumna Canal is 
not suitable for floating on account of tho numerous locks and falls, and it is estimated 
that to render it navig tble for timber rafts 27 locks would have to be constructed, and 
that the cost would be about five or six lakhs of rupees. 

105. Arrangement with the Sirmur State.—One of the difficulties connected with 
the floating of sleepers to Delhi is the fact that the greater portion of the western 
bank of the Tons is notin British territory, and in consequence timber thefts are 
numerous. Thus in 1884-85 1,167 sleepers were stolen, and in the following year 
2,043. The boom at Dakbpatthar is fixed to a rock on the Sirmiur side of the Jumna, 
and this together with the fict that the Mahardja has a certain length of river frontage 
along the Tons and Jumna has induced him to claim one-third the river dues which 
aro collected at Dakhpatthar, In G. O. No. 629F., dated 21st November, 1887, to 
Commissioner of Meerut, the Local Governmont admit the justice of his claim bat 
state that it is not their intention to make a profit at the boom. The Mahfr&jah’s 
claim is basod on the following. 

(1) For drift wood not marked, which has been valued at Rs. 40 to Rs. 50 
per annum. 

(2) For the establishment he himself entertains for the supervision of thefts on 
thd river, which he puts at Ks. 200 to Rs. 300. 


(3) For rent for the rock at Dakhpatthar to which the boom is attached. 
(4) For right of way along the banks of the Tons river in his territory. 


The Lieutenant-Governor considered that these claims would be liberally met by 
an annual allowanco of Rs. 590, or by one-third of tho bvom dues, whichever is the 


greater sum in any your.

107. Blocks. —The Lambatach forest was divided into blocks and the Deota, 
Bamsu, Sardis and Kotigad forests into Llucks and compartments by my predecessors 
Mr. Rind, I believe, duing most of the work. It would perhaps have been best to have 
given serial numbers to all the blocks and to divide the circle in a somewhat different 
manner, but as maps of the Lambatéch and Deota forests had already been printed 
showing all the blocks and compartinents, it was thonght bettor not to make any alter- 
ation but to uso the same system in the division of the remaining forests, 


108. Compartments.— Regarding each of the Lambat&ch blocks as a compartment, 
there are in all 165 of these divisions varying in size from 31 to 2,8CO acres, but 
usually between 100 and 300 acres the very large ones containing an area stocked with 
oaks and firs, which it would be purposeless to further divide. The comprrtments 
in the Deotn forests have had pillars erected around their boundaries and are, asa 
rule, smaller than the ones formed by me in the newly-demarcated areas where I tried 
as mach as possible too avoid too minute a sub-division. The manner in which they 
have been formed can be seen from the maps and from the description of their contents, 
and here it need only be said that the deodar zone has been separated as much as 
possible from the karshu and chir zones. 


Valuation surcey.— Lambatach forest having been worked out, no valuation of its 
contents has been made. Most of the Deota forest was valued by Mr. Rind in 1877-79. 
Its valuation was completed by Mr. Lowrie in 1879-80, when he also valued the Bamsu 
forest and part of the Kotig&d forest, finishing its valuation as well as that of the 
Saras forest in 2880-81. 


The mode in which this valuation was made is described by Mr. Rind‘as follows, (but 
it was not correct to take the mean girth, the number of trees, as may be seen from

for trees 3-6 feet in girth, 4 feet; for trees from 6-9 teet ia girth, 7 foet ; for trees from 
9-12 feet in guth, LO feet would represent the average.)

The girths are at four feet from the ground. The heights refer to the length of 
marketable stem, such as will yield logs for 10 feot sleepers, and not to tha total 
height of the tree, The system of enuincration by tickets was adopted as follows :— 


Four colors were selected fur the girth classes, viz , olass [ white, clasa [I green, 
class JIL red, and class LV blue. The height classos were denoted as follows 
on these tickets: the lowest height class by a plain ticket, and the remaining 
three height classes by the Nos 1, 2 and 3, respectively, printed on the tickets. 
Round tissue paper tickets two inches in diameter, prepared as above, were procured 
from Roorkee, and fastened up in bundles of 100 each. The plan pursued in valuing 
a forest by the ticket systcin is as follows, the officer in chargo organising each party 


thus :— 
A fo.ester in charge of three sub-parties, cach of the following strength :— 


1 Forest guard. 
© Geugcers. 
8 lLleigh¢ measnrers and ticketers, 


To the forester he makes over a certain number of tickets very carefully counted, 
also the gaugos, the bamboos, the paste pots and bags (in which there are 13 pockets 
for the tickets, and which are slung over the gaugers’ backs), Mach sub-party passes 
through the forest in a line of pairs, each pair consisting of a gaugor and height 
measurer, the forest guard directing them. The diameter of each tree is measured with 
a gauge, and at the same time its height is estimated by the aid of a 20 feet bamboo 
placed against the trunk. Ou vach tree so measured a ticket of the color and number 
representing its girth and height class is affixed on a blaze cut to receive it. The forest 
guards are responsible for the quantity of work done. Tho forester keeps a journal 
showing the daily work of his sub-parties, notes the issues and receipts of tickets, 
and exercises constant sapervision by moving about in the forest, watching the wurk, 
taking check girths and heights in the completed portions, and recording the same in 
his diary. The check by the officer in charge consists in his periodically visiting the 
work, testing it on lines through each compartment if he suspects inaccuracy, and 
by occasionally resurveying a whole compartment, 

Mr. Brandis considered the classification needlessly elaborate, and considered it 
sufficient to count the doodar trees of two classes abovo six feet in girth and between 
three and six feet in girth, whilst the average length of the first class trees and the 
number of sleepers they will yield can readily be estimated for each compartment, 
The valuation af the remaining forests was undertaken by myself at the time [I 
‘demarcated them in 1885 udder the terms of the new lease. The same system (Mr. 
Dansey’s) in making the enumoration was adopted as that empl syed in the Nindhaur 
valley and in the Gauges Division, and which is explained in the working plans for 
those forests.

Most of the trees are sound, and in the forests recently valued the unsound trees 


did not amount to 2 per cent. of the totul number of I class trees. Inthe Dopta block 
of the Leota forest, however, which is rocky and precipitous, many trees from 32 to 38 
inches in diameter were found to be hollow in the centre, whilst in the adjoining ‘lem- 
ple block where the soil is deep and good, trees of 4U inches in diameter and 250 years 


old were quite sound. 

113 It is evident from the above figures that the deodar is here capable of 
reaching a girth of at least from 6 to 9 feet and the question is at what size do they 
reach their maximum of atijity. This must be considered solely from the outturn of 
eleepers which is at present almost the only marketable commodity. Experiments have 
been made at Deota to determine this outturn per tree, sample trees being chosen to 
represent different girths and the bost sawyers being employed to saw them up, the 
rate of pay being higher and greater care taken than with ordinary trees. Particulars 
of these measurements ure given in Appendix 1, but thoy are somewhat discordant and 
the form in which the results are shown having been changed almost every year, it is 
very difficult to make any useful deductions and owing to want of supervision the 
figures are not very reliable. From this table, however, we gather that the outturn

The falling off of is very noticeable. It is attributed to the infvrior size and 
auality of the trees now being felled, to greater strictness in excluding sapwood from 
all sleepers prepared, and perhaps to the 1ochy nature of the fellings and consequent 
breakages. It was found that tho porcentago of breakages was greater when trees 
were felled by the saw than when felled by the axe, and Mr. Moir considers the for 
mer method unsuitable on steep ground as it is more difficult to direct the fall of “the 
tree than when the axe is used. In 1883-84 whon the outturn was 64 per sleeper, 
fellings were made in Katatdéch 3 and 4 and iv Jaurdsi 3 and 4, compartments not 
so superior to Jaurasi 4, 5 and 6, where fellings have lately taken place, as to justify 
the great diminution in the outturn which is more likely due to the marking of a 
number of undersized trees, but no particulars can be obtained on this point. Contin- 
ing our feltings as it is proposed to do to trees over two fuet in diameter the yield per 
treo may be placed at 50 metre gauge slecpers as the average for the whole of the 
forests, inclusive of sleepers obtained from dry wood.

it has not been found possible cither to get md of this wood or to utilise it profitably.

to be constructed, the undertaking can only provo remunerative if conducted un 
largo scale, whilst it is important to utilise the slides, sledge roads, &o , to the fulle 
extent possible. Taking into consideration the length of the working season, the time 
required for sledging, sliding, &c., 1t 18 bolieved that as regards the majority of the 
forests a lakh of sleepers is the maximum that can be worked out in a year from one 
base of operations and a single system of oxport works. The inadvisability of having 
continuous departmental works 1n two places is proved by tho situation and state of 
the forests it 18 intended to leave in reserve,

In the Kotigad forest the trees availablo are scattered over a very large area 
and are situated for the most part at a considerable distance from a floatable streuip, 
the Gokal block being at the head of the Bann4li g4d many miles from its junction 
with the Kotigfd, at which point only could the sleepers be floated to the Pabar. The 
present price of sleepors scarcely warrants making expensive export works for bring- 
ing out the small quantity of timber available. ‘he Chansil forest has already been 
partially worked, and when the two thousand additional trees which it is proposed to 
fell in the Andri and Boktaéra géds have been extracted, all the finest trees in this 
forest will have disappeared. Lastly the Nordinu forest is situated above a line of 
precipices, much of the forest itself being very precipitous, so that it is by no means 
cortain that sleepers can be profitably exported; at present, therefore, it seems unne- 
cessary to draw np a regular scheme for working these forests, whilst it is very useful 
to have a reserve to fall back on in case of need. If, however, during the course of 
the lease, there happens to be ‘a pressing demand for a larger oulturn of sleepers and 
the working scheme for the othor forests is progressing according to the programme, 
the reserve forests can be indented on for the extra supply, or standing trees can bo 
sold from them to contractors, provided that such a course in no way interfered with 
the slecpers worked out departmentally. Thus if the department is bringing out 
narrow gauge sleepers it might be stipulated that the purchaser of standing deodar 
should export his timber in the form of broad gauge sleepers or of scauntling of various 
dimensions. 


121. Protection from fire and grazing.—Al) the forests being already fire-protected, 
there is nothing to prescribe under this head. As to grazing it is not contemplated 
at present to close any further areas, as the forests are mostly remote from villages, 
and the grazing is therefore very light, the most valued portions of the forests for this 


( 37 ) 


purpose, being, moreover, the zone of karsxhu and firs above the deodér which is at 
present otherwise valueless. The forest ofticer should, however, have uo difticulty in 
closing any compartments where, hereafter, cultural considerations show such a course 
to be necessary, 


122. Piantations —As the larger part «f these forests has only been fire-protected 
since 1085, the resuly of such protection must be awaited before 1t can be determined 
what areas it will be necessary to plint up artificially ; but judging from the results of 
fire-protection 1u the Deota and ambatach forests, it is hoped that it will be uune- 
cessary to resort to plunting operations on a lirge scale. ‘There are, however, certain 
areas in the forests which have been protected fur ten yeira or more, where it 1s 
hopeless to expect a natural crop of deo 4r seodlings owing either to the absence of seed- 
bearers orto other causes. It is, therefore, proposed to plant up the following areas 
during the period of this working plan a ‘cording to the scheme given Lelow, endeavours 
being made to plant or sow up as nearly as possible 40 acres yearly with deodér in 
lines of pits 6’ x6’ but this will, of course, depend on the quantity of seed available.

The 500 acres which it is proposed to stock with deoldr in the Kotigéd forest 
are most favorably situated for the purpose, and the planting operations should prove 
a gioat success ‘The aspect is more or less northerly, the ground steepish but not 
rocky or precipitous, whilst the young kail and ferns will afford a most welcome 
shelter to the young deodir. The pits can first be sown up and nurseries established 
close at hand to supply plants for filling up failures. In the Chakora and Banarpéni 
blocks strong healthy three-year old plants should be used and tie pits will have to be 
most curefully drained. Pipal 4 is the only coinpartment where it will be necessary 
to sow young kail before planting deoddr. During the dry season the seedlings will 
have to be carefully protected from the sun by grass cowls in places where they do 
not get sufficient shelter from young kail or other plants, special care being taken op 
waim southorly aspects.

division, it is unnecessary to expatiate here on the methods to Le used in sowing and
