123. Cultural operations prior to the fellings.—With the fact before us that 
deodar is the only tree of any value in these forests, the importance of aiding it in its 
struggle for existence with its neighbours is self-vvident. Probably the best and 
chenpest means of rendering this assistance is by a continuance of the operationa 
described in paragraphs 75 and 76, only doing so according to a regular plan instead 
of here and there in a desultory kind of way as has hitherto been the case. The scheme 
given below has been so arranged thnt the thinnings shall precede the selection 
fellings usually by five years. The operations to be carricd out are those detailed by 
Mr. Fernandez in paragraph 80 of his working plan for the Mundali forest in this 
division, and which it would be superfluous to repeat here. It will, however, probably 
not be feasible to carry out completely his rule No. 1V over such largo areas, but every 
endeavour should be made to adhere as closely as possible to the three first rulcs, 
The areas to be worked over annually may appear excessive, but it should be recol- 
Jected that a portion only of each block will really require thinning, although owing 
to the absence of maps it is impossible to indicate even approximately the acreage 
which will kave to be gone over each year, but taking into account blanks and other 
causes it will not probably amount to more than one-third of the deodar producing 
area.

The principles on which this operation should be conducted are explained in 
paragraph 105 of the Mundali working plan but the thinning should be more severe 
than as laid down in the paragraph roferred to, since the young deodér can stand any 
quantity of light, provided it receives a certain amount of lateral protection, whilst

ground at very short intervals, As ringing operations have already been made over a 
considerablo portion of the Deota forest, the thinnings prescribed above will probably

fully watched, and if necessary further cleanings will have to be made, but it is not

The ranger with all the sleeper works on his hands, will have but littlo time to 
attend to theso cultural operations, and in order that they may be properly carried out, 
it is suggested to engage five parties similar to those employed on enumeration sarveys, 
and if possible the same men. Each party would consist of seven khalasis and one 
jamadir and would have to work for two or three months in the year, (April, May, and 
June). The divisional officer and the ranger can instruct the jamadars, who are intel- 
ligent men, in the mode of conducting these operations, and after a little guidance they 
will duubtless do the work required of thom in a very satisfactory manner. 


125, Meport Works.—It is not intended to give hero carefully prepared plans of 
the export and other works which will be required to carry out this working plan, but 
only to offer some general hints on the subject. The reasons tor this may be briefly 
stated. 

(1) To prepare plans and estimates of all the works required for opening out the 
forests, the working plans officer would require at least a whole working season and 
the publication of the present plan would be greatly delayed. 


(2) The nature of the works depends to a great exteut on the demand for timber 
of definite dimensions, so that if, for instance, as has been mooted, the Rajputana Rail- 
way system were to be converted from the narrow to tho broad gauge, most of the 
plans and estimates would become useless, 


(3) It is to be hoped that improvements in the methods of hill transport will con- 
tinue to dovelor, so that by postponing the preparation of plans and estimates until 
the works are actually required, full advantage can bo taken of all now ideas, 


126, Under these circumstances it will be best that proposals for constructing 
export works such as roads, slides, sledge roads aud so forth be drawn up for each 


( 40 ) 


separate export basin two years previous to the beginning of felling operations. Four 
different schemes will thus have to be prepared approximately at the following dates.

These schemes being thus ready {wo vears in advance, many export works such 
as the construction of roads, making streams floatable, &., can be put in hand as soon 
ax sanctioned, thus avoiding delay and interruption when passing from one eaport basin 
to another. In drawing up these schemes the system ef transport to be used must, of 
course, depend alinost entirely on the relative cheapness of the transport employed 
compared with the expenditure tv be incurred. ‘Tu enable a comparison to be drawn 
between the difforent systews at present in use, the following rates per sleeper per 
mule show the actual cost of cxporting metre vauge sleepers from the Deota forest.

The cust per mile does not vary much whatever the length of the export lines if

From these figures it will be soen that, in order to recover the cost, about 14 
Jakhs of sleepers would have to pass over the sledge road and about two lakhs would 
hive tu pass down tha slide, so that it would not pay to make such export works for a 
smaller number of sleepers. In the case where either a wet slide or a sledge roud 
could be made, tahing the same figures, wo fiud that the former, if only one mile long, 
would not be so remunerative as the latter unless 63 lakhs of sleepers couid be passed 
down it, whereas if the length were two wiles it would pay better than a sledge road 
with an export of 4 lakhs, and for a length of three miles with an export of 33 lakhs only, 


The nature of the export works that will probably be required nny be now briefly 
pointed out. 


127. Deota.—The sledge road is new and in good order, so that only slight 
repairs will be required. The wet slide, on the contrary, is old and very leaky, ani 
extensive repairs wil] have to be made. The largest number of sleepers hitherto brought 
down in one season bas been about 50,000 only, and in order to export double that 
quantity it will be necessary to stop the great leakage of tho slide, rendering it more 
watertight by caulking the seams with tow or sunn and tar or pfl resin (141) and by 
making a few dams in the bed of the stream, in order to bring moie water into use 
so as to prolong the floating season; it may also be necessary to work at night. If 
this does not suffice the slide will have to be doubled. It is perhaps possible to make 
tar on the spot, and this should be tried. 


128. Bameu and Sards —In June, 1885, Mr. Moir inspected the Bamsu gid with 
the object of ascertaining whether it would be possible to construct a slide for the 
export of timber from these forests. The anuexed sketch map (tracing No, 6) accom- 
panied his report, which may be summarised thus. “ It is to be hoped that some 
system of wire tramway will be invented in order to got the timber past the waterfull 
and precipice which blocks the way about two miles from the Tons. Owing to the 
distance of the lower portion of the slide from the Bameu forest, it will be out of the 
question to use deodar for the construction of the slide on acconnt of the cost of carriage, 
so that chir timber will have to be employed, but the main beams of the bridges 
and ‘secunjus’ or block sleepers can be made of hill toon and other woods which are 
available in the gad. The construction of the proposed slide from the Tons as far as 


( 41 ) 


the precipica will present no difficulty, as there is only one small waterfall, and the 
average gradient is not more than 5 or 6 degrees. For the upper portion of the gid 
above the waterfall the ground is practicable for either a slide or a sledge road, but 
the former is, of course, preferable, since ample water power is available, and timber 
tor this portion can be obtained from the demarcated area.” 


From the felling acheme it will be observed that about 4} lakhs of sleepers are 
expected to come from the two forests, so that a slida from the foot of the waterfall to 
the Tons should prove a remunerative work. From the Bamsu forest above three lakhs 
of sleepers should be brought out, and a sledge road from the forest to the head of the 
waterfall would pay better thana slide, unless the latter could be constructed for less 
money than estimated in paragraph 126.From the Sards forest 14 lakhs of sleepers are 
expected, an da sledge road would therefore only just pay its way; so that unless it can he 
imade very cheaply it would be better to employ coolies to bring the sleepers to the slide. 


129. Sdhlra.—This forest is situated at about a mean distance of 7 miles from the 
Tons, and the export would be down the Kuni gad, which the neighbouring villagers 
say is floatable during the rains below the forest boundary for a distance of about 5 
miles ; but this stream has not been examined during that season, and the distance to the 
Tons is estimated only. If not, however, at present floatable, it can probably be easily 
rendered so, at least in the lower part of its course. Slides could be nade down all the main 
tributaries, but it would not probably pay to muke any even in the Kuni, Sili or 
Jodar gfds, as only one or one and a-half lakhs of sleepers would come from each 
basin ; sleepers from the Kuni, Dw&nti and Sili gdds would all havo to be brought 
to a point just above the line of precipices, and it is possible that at this point it 
might be worth while to construct a dry slide or a wire tramway to carry them down 
to the wet slide below. The works, however, which will almost certainly have to be 
undertaken, will be a wet slide from the foot of the precipice to the point from which 
it is possible to make the Kuni gfd fluatable, and from this point tothe Tons, works in 
the bed of the stream to facilitate floating the sleepers. It is expected that 54 lakhs 
of sleepers will come from this forest. 


130. Natntwdr.—This forest is situated directly over the Tons at a mean distance 
of less than three miles from the river. There are two separate export basins, tis : 
the Mairi and Koarbo gfds, and owing to the small number of sleepers, two lakhs 
from the whole forest, it is probable that special export works would prove unremune- 
rative, and that coolie labor will have to be relied on for bringing the sleepers to the river, 


131. Kotiydd.—Both the Andri and Boktara gads flow into the Koti géd, which 
is floatable below its junction with the Audri gad, but sleepers often take as long as 
three or four months before they reach the P&bar, which is only distant about 11 
miles, and it would therefore seem advisable to spend sume money in improving the 
be: of the stream by removing the chief obstacles. 


The Gokal block of this forest is about threo miles from the Koti g&d, the line of 
export being down the Banndli gad. Ifa slide were made from the Murali giid to the 
main stream, a distance of about 1} miles, it would tap this block as well as Baloha and 
Mur4li. It is doubtful, however, considering the quantity of timber availyble, whether 
such a slide would pay ; but as the forest is to be kept in reserve, the question is not 
an important one for the present. Sleepers from the Ola block would have to be carried 
direct to the Koti gad, the mean length of carriage being a little over 3 miles. 


132. Nordnu and Ddtmir.—The Kanoni gid, which runs through the Norénu 
forest, reaches the Rupin just above its junction with the Tons, after a course of about 
three miles ; but owing to the small quantity of mature timber, it would not probably 
be worth while making a slide, although its construction is perfectly feasible. Bring- 
ing the sleepers past the line of precipitous ground will, however, be a very dificult 
undertaking ; and if only some workable kind of wire tramway could be devised, this 
would be the very place to try it in. It is not, however, proposed to work this forest 


during the present lease. 
11 


( 42 ) 


Datmir being on the Tons itself, no expensive export works will be required even 
if it is deterwined to make any fellings here during the next 17 years, There are, 
however, few good trees at present available, and no fellings are provided for in the 
plan. 


133. Communications and buildings.—The roads, bridges, and buildings enume- 
rated in paragraphs 80-85 will havo to be kept in repair, except tho sleeper-carrying 
paths in the blocks which have been worked out, and the Maindrdt suspension bridge 
and bungalow which are no longer required. A year before the selection fellings in the 
Deota forest are brought to a conclusion, it will be useful to make a good bridle path 
to Bumsu by widening the present path from Deota, and a similar path will have to be 
made later on from Deota to Sahira along the main ridge, a short one joining it from 
Bamsu. Roads passable for pack animals carrying food for the sawyers and coolies 
must also be made along the right bank of the Tons from Tadfér, passing by the mouth 
ef the Bamsu gad to that of the Kuni g4d dowu which will come all timber from 
the Saéhira forest ; whilst branches will have to be made up the two gf&ds referred to, 
and also one from Naiutwar bridge to Koarbo, the road on the left bank of tho ‘Tons as 
far as that bridge being widened and im; roved. 


Forest rest-houses and servants’ quarters will havo to be built at the camping- 
ground iu the Séhira forest and at the mouth of the Kuni gad, whilst the Bamsu hut 
must be enlarged and improved. Houses for the forest ranger and chaukis for forest 
guards wiil be required at Bamsu, Sdhira, Kuni g4d, and Naintwér; godowus for the 
food-supply will also have to be built at Bamsu, Sahira, and Naintwar. 


13%. The artificial boundaries of the newly-demarcated forests should be clearly 
defined by a cut path two feet wide as in the case of the Deota forests. It is estimat- 
ed that forty miles of paths will be required, aud this might be carried out at the rate 
of from five to ten miles yearly,

There is thus au expected surplus of a little over Rs. 54,000 per annum, or an 
annual profit from the deod&r-producing area of over Rs. 1-13-0 fer acre, and this 
after making a most liberal allowance for all possible expenditure ; no standing trees 
should be seld from these forests for less than Rs. 85 each, unless perhaps from the 
Nor&nu forest, where the export is particularly difficult, or from tho Gokal block, which 
is rather remote from a floatable stream. 


( 44 ) 


136. With the aid of the above figures, it may be best to explain here why it is 
more profitable to carry on these works departmentally rather than to sell stunding 
trees, as the reasons do not appear as yet to be well understood or appreciated. 


The expenditure, which would not be incurred if the treeg were sold to contractors, 


may be estimated as follows :— mat 
eis Ra. 

A. I G. ece eee eee eet ese eae eae 11,665,009 
A. V1 6 chalf of) ae = sae = vee 1,700 
A. VII a (construotion of export patha and miscellaneous repaira) cee 4,400 
B I.a,6 and d Two-thirds deducted from exyenditure incarred bal 1.54.200 
B. Ii. a, 6,c and d departmental woking. ili 

Total soe eee §=213,95,400 


Deducting this amount from the total expenditure of Rs. 16,82,445, we have 
a balance of Rs. 3,57,045, which would represent the expenditure if departmental 
works were abolished. In order, therefore, to obtain a sarplus of Us. 9,18,555, we 
should have to sell our 34,000 trees for Rs. 12,75,600, or over Rs. 37 per tree. 
Hitherto the highest price obtained for standing deodfr has been Rs. 11-8 0 for some 
trees in the Andri gfid within two miles of a floatable stream, so that it is clearly out 
of the question to expect to realize throe times that amount for trees the scantling 
from which would have to be curried five or six miles by means of costly export works 
to the Tons. 


The ordinary purchasers of standing trees have not sufficient knowledge to con- 
struct sach works, which are of a special character, the details of their construction 
forming not an unimportant part in the training of forest officers; and if they could 
be made by private agency it is doubtful whether any purchasers would be willing 
to invest so much capital in the forests. Ou the other hand, if these works were made 
departmentally, the price per tree would be much enhanced; and whilst private indivi- 
duals can obtain deod&r from the forests of the hill chiefs at low rates, they are not 
likely to ofter much higher sums for the privilege of working in the Government forests. 
If, therefore, it is considered desirable to encourage private enterprise in this instance, 
it can only be done by sacrificing nearly the whole of the anticipated surplus.

5th September, 1887. Deputy Conservator of Forests, Working Plana 


APPENDIX A. 


LEASE OF TEHRI FORESTS. 


1, Toe Raja Partéb Séh of Tehri hereby places at the disposal of the British 
Government the deodar forests in tlle Tons and Pabar valleys and the Sheopuri forest 
within his territory, the boundaries of which are sot out in the schedule annexed. No 
person save the said Government shall have the right to take timber out of the said 
forests ; but any privileges hitherto enjoyed by the ryots of taking wood for building 
houses, for burning, for makmy plouglis, and generally for agricultural purposes, 


shall continue intact. 


2. The said Government is authorised to take steps for the more complete 
conservation of any portion of the forests hereby placed at its disposal. The said 
Government may prohibit the grazing of cattle or opening of thoroughfares therein, 
with due regard to the existing privileges of villages which have hithorto exercised 
the right of grazing; and the Conservator of [Forests may fiom time to time issue such 
further orders, with the approval of the Raja, as he may think proper for the moro 
effectual protection of the trees. 


8. No porson shall be entitled to cloar ground for building in the forests refer- 
red to in clauso 2, nor shall any one be permitted to cultivate on the cleared ground 
in tho midst of such forests. 


4 The Raja shall aid to the utmost of his power in the protection of the forests 
and in the punishment of persons infringing the forest rules and 1.egulations, 


5, Tho rules and regulations laid down by the Conservator in regard to the 
forests, having been approved by the Raja, shall be enforced, and the Raja shall regard 
any infringement of those rules as an oftunce and shall puaish the offender. 


6. The Réja shall be competent to take whatever timber he may need for his 
personal use or for the private 1equirements of lus family. 


7, Tho British Government shall be entitled to make roads which may require 
to pass through fields containing standing crops, but shall be bound to make compen- 
sation for the loss thereby inflicted. On such compensation being paid, the Raja will 
not object to the construction of the road. 


8. The sum of Rs. 12,000 shall be paid yearly by the British Government in 
fulfilment of tho above recorded provisions as consideration for the lease. 


9. The above recorded provisions shall hold good for twenty years from the lst 
May, 1885, and on the expiry of that poriod the British Government shall be entitled 
to renew it for a further term; but the consideration for such subsequent lease shall 
bo determined by mutual agreomont and consent of both parties (namely, of the Bui- 
tish Government and of the Raja of Teli) and based upon the fair value of the 
timber to be annually taken out, 


One copy of this agreement, after it has been signed by or on behalf of both the 
contracting parties, shall be deposited with the British Government and anothor copy 
shall be kept by the Réja.

APPENDIX F. 


RIVER RULES. 
(Extracts from G. 0. No. 300 ve 28'h February, 1880, 1004 of Ind November, 


1880 and from No, a 26th June, 1885 ) 


29—h0? 


No. 300.—In accordance with tho provisions of section 45 of the Indian Forest 
Act, VII of 1878, the Hon’ble the Lieutenant-Governor, North-Western Provinces, 
and Chief Commissioner, Oudh, is pleased to direct that the following shall be the 
areas within which all unmarked wood and timber shall be deemed the property of 
Government, unless and until uny person establishes his right and title thoreto:— 


(a) All reserved and protected forests in the North-Wostern Provinces and 
Oudh. 

(b) The Jumna and its tributaries in British territory above the Scinde, Panjab, 
and Delhi Railway bridgo at Rajghat, including an area of three miles from either 
bank of the main stream of the Jumna river, taking that stream at its cold season 
level 
(c-f.) other streams follow not connected with the Jaunsar Division. 


The Lieutenant-Governor and Chief Commissioner is further pleased to exempt 
from the provisions of section 45 of the Iudian Forest Act, 1878, all head-loads of 
drift, timber of evory description; the pieces not to excoced 6’ in length or 2’ in girth. 

No, 1004.—Under seotion 41, Act VII of 1878 (tho Indian Forest Act', the 
Lientenant-Governor of the North Western Provinoes and Chief Commisioner of Oudh 
has made, and with the sanotion of the Governor-General in Council is pleased to 
publish, the following rales for the reserved and protected forosts*in the North-\Ves- 
tern Provinces and Oudh :—= 

(1) All words used in these rules and defined in Act VII of 1878 (the Indian 
Forest Act) shall be deemed to have the meaning respectively attributed to them by 
the said Act. 

9-5 I.—Transport by land (details omitted here). 


Lannching and collec- 6. Il.—Transport by river.—No person having launched 
tion of timber. his timber, or set it afloat on any river, shall collect the same, 
except— 


(a) With the permission in writing of the Forest Officer in charga of the Forest 
Division. 

(b) At places where such officer shall notify as catching depdts, 

7, No person shall raft or otherwise convey any timber which has been collected 
on any river without first obtaining a pass from the Forest 
Officer in charge of the Forest Division in which the river is 
situated, or from such subordinate officer as tho officer in chargo of the Forest Division 
may authorize on that behalf. Such pass shall show the number of logs or pieces, 
the kind of timber, the marks indicative of the owner’s property thorein, the place 
of its destination, and the time for which it shull remain in force. It may specify 
that the timber may be stopped in cortain places to be named in the pass. 


Pass for rafting timber. 


No pass to be issued 8. No pass shall be issued for any unmarked timber, or 
iB sige pacer for such timber as bears a mark not registered as hereinafter 
mark, provided. 

9. Any Forest Officer may require any person rafting or conveying timber 
foresaid, i 
janine Se to produce the pass for the same at any time. 
default of production of No person,shall be entitlod to raft or convey timber by virtue 
pase. of a pass which he does not himself hold, hut which is, or is 
stated to be, in the hands of some other person. In the event of the pass not being 
produced, the Forest Ofticer may detain the timber.

10. For issue of the pass a fee not not exceeding one anna for each log or piece 
of timber may ba lovied on such river, and at such places, as 


Fee for Issue of pass. ; ; 
: : the Conservator of Forests may from time to timo direct, 


11. All persons wishing to float or otherwise convoy timber by any river shall 
register, at the offico of the Forest Division to which tho control 
of the river pertains, the mark which indicates the proprietary 
right in the timber. A fee of 2s. 5 shall be payable for such registration. Not more 
than one mark shull bo registered for each owner. 


Repivtration. 


12. No person shall be allowed to registor a mark already registered in favour of 
Refusal to rogieter cer- 8Nother person, nor any mark used by Government ; and the 
tain wurks. Forest Officer may refuse the registration of any mark which in 
his judgement so closely resembles a mark used by Government or registored in 
favour of any person as to be oasily produciblo by altering such marks. 


13. Every rogistration under these rules shall bold good for three years, 


Registration to hold following the Ist of January next after the date of registra- 
good for three years, tion. 


A certificate showing the mark rogistered, the date of registration, the period fur 
Certificate of registra. Which it holds good and acknowledging the payment of tho fee, 
ma shall be given to every person registering his mark. 


14. Within the limits of any area notified under section 45, the converting, 
cutting, burning, concealing, or marking of timber, the altering or effacing of any 
marks on the same, and tho possession or carrying of marking hammers or other 
implements used for the marking of timber, except with the permission in writing of 
the Forest Officer, is prohibited. Such permission, if granted, shall specify the place 
at which only it is to take effect, and may contain such other conditions regarding 
the previous inspection of the timber and otherwise as may be necessary. 


15. III. General_—Any person who infringes any of these rules shall be liable 
to imprisonment of either description for a term which may 
extend to six months, or to fine which may extend to fivo 
hundred rupees or to both.

APPENDIX G. 


eEmmsereasilig) 


NOTICK., 


Tie following arrangements are made by the Conservator of Forests, School 
Circle, North-Westorn Protinces, for the transport of timber on the river Juinna, 
They have reference to Section II. of Government Notification No. 1004, Rovenue 
(Forests) Department, dated 2nd November, 1880 : — 


1, Property mark—Any person wishing to use the river for floating his timber 
must register his property mark in the office of the Tons (now Jaunsar) Forest Division. 
He will obtain:a certified extract from the register book, on pnyment of a registration 
fee of Rs 5. No. more than one mark can be registered for each owner. * Application 
to register a mark may be made cither personally or by letter to the Officer in charge 
of the Tons Forest Division The registration holds good for three years following the 
Ist January neat alter the date of registration, 


9. Collection of timber—At least one month before the timber is launched, the 
owner must apply to the Officer in charge of the Tons Forest Division for permission 
to collect it. Tho application must be in writing, and must contain the fullowing 
information, vit.s— 

(1) Name and address of owner. 

(2, His father’s name, 


(3) Description of the timber, including the number and dimensions of the 
pieces, 


(4) Property mark which it bears. 


(5) The period within which the timber is to reach Déghpathar. 


On receipt of this application the Officer in charge of the Tons Forest Division will 
issue an “collecting pass” fice of charge. Government maintains a boom and un

tute a suit for the recovery of possession of tho timber, the timber will 
become the proper of Government. “ae

in writing, of the Oliicer in chargo of the Tous Forest Division, is prohibited.

APPENDIX H, 
Tux WESTERN JUMNA CANAL Routes ror THE MEASUREMENT oF Rarts. 


1. Rarts composed partly of squared and partly of round timber, or of logs of 
very unequal sizes will not be allowed to enter the canal. 


2. All rafts after being made up will be moved to tho right bank in tho firat 
mile of the canal, where they will be measured, and aftor issue of rawannahs and pay- 
ment of dues will be passed down the canal. 


3. No raft to moasure more than 18 feet across, that is, in a direction at right 
anglos to that in which tho rafts will float. 


4, The contents of log rafts will be calculated as follows :— 
Contents.—§rds of length X breadth x depth. 


By length is to be understood the dimension of the raft in the direction of the 
logs, the average length of which, as judged by the eye, is to be taken. 


The breadth is the dimeusion measured across the logs, from outside to outside, 
The depth will be formed by dividing the breadth by the number of logs. 
When rafts are composed of logs of very differont length they may be 
measured in two or more parts, the contents of cach part being taken out 
separately and added together for the contonts of the whole raft. 


5. Bamboo lashings on log rafts to be measured as follows :—= 
Contents. —Number of bundles x length of a bundle X 5 of the square of its girth. 


If the length or girths of the bundlos vary considerably, the contents of the 
bundles to be taken out separately. 


8. Bamboo rafts.—The contents to be measured in the same way as directed for 
log rafts, cach bundle being regarded as a Jog. 


7. Rafts of sleepers or squared timber.—The contents of a sleeper or squared 
timber raft, exclusive of lashings, will be calculated as follows :— 


Contents, Longth X breadth x depth. 


The contents may be taken out in parts when necossary, the contents of the 
whole raft being the sum of the parts. The sleepers used as lashings 
will be counted and tho contents of the lashings found by multiplying 
the number by tho measured contents of one sleeper, The number used 
as lashings will bo always noted in the rawannah. 


8, Karis and other small scantling, if tied in bundles, will be moasured in the 
same way a8 bamboo rafts. If luid side by side the contents of the rafts and lashings 
to be measured in tho same way as slceper rafts 

9, Ballah rafts will bo measured in the same way as logs rafts, 

10. Grass, sirkanda and firewood.—The contents will be the product of the 
actual length, breadth and depth as measured. 

11. When rafts of any kind are composed of two or more courses, the measure 
ments will be made separately for each course, in accordance with foregoing instruc« 
tions, the total contents being the sum of those of the sepatate courses.

Nole by the Assistant Inspector-Generul of Foresla and Superintendent of Working-Plans on 
Mr, Hearle’s Working-Plan for the Tehri-Garhwal Leased Forests. 


Tae summary of this Plan, sabmitted by the Conservator of the School 
Circle, under cover of his No. 296, dated the 19th November 1887, is sufficiently 
exhaustive to render any very elaborate description of its object and provisions 
superfluous. 


2. The first six chapters of Mr. llearle’s work relate to such general 
matters as are necessary to a proper comprehension of local conditions, and 
contain, in this connection, a great variety of usclul, as also necessary, in- 
formation admirably arranged. 

Chapter VIL ineludes the Working-Plan proper, and Chapter VIII the 
financial forecast constructed on the same. A number of appendices and 
maps and plans follow, bearing ou matters cantained in the Report. 


8. This Working-Plan is intended to reeulate the working of the Deodar 
tracts leased to us by the Rajah of Tehri-Garhwal for the period commencing 
with the Ist April 1885 and ending with the 31st March 1905, and, as such, 
is made to embrace the period of that Icase only, namely, 20 years. 


4. The area of these leased forests is 45,198 acres, of which only 29,660 
aeres are said to be Deodar-produeing ; aud the Plan relates fa (his one species 
only, and to the working and management of the forests in its exclusive 
interest; the other species met with having no present market value, or being 
likely to have any within the period of the Icase. 


5. Tater on, it is probable that these leased forests—since the British 
Government has the right to renew the lease—will be comprised, with certain 
neighbouring forests, in one Working-Cirele, and be rendered subject to a more 
comprehensive and permanent Working-Plan. In his introduction, Mr. Iearle 
observes in this connection :—* The present Plan, when it prescribes that the 
Tehri leased forests shall be worked through in 20 years, regards them as 
forming a portion only of a large Working-Cirele, the precise composition of 
which cannot be determined until the remaining Deodar forests in the Jaunsdr 
Division have heen valued. In fact, there seems no goo reason for treating 
them apart. from the other forests, as the British Government has the right to 
renew the lease for a further term at the expiry of the present lease in 1905.” 


G. This Plan, therefore, mainly aims at regulating the extraction of 
Deodar froin a given area during a short term of years, and in such a way 
as, to give us the largest profits compatible with our responsible position of 
lessees of the annual yield only, and of temporary enardians entrusted with 
the office of combining our working with such improvements as appear to be 
ealled for. 

7. The valuation survey for these leased forests shows :— 


I class Deodar (6° girth and over) re ..» 137,000 trees, 
Il » ong) (OY 6") ids sis . 50,000 _,, 


But Mr, JTearle gives 46,000 I class sound green trecs as being alone avail- 
able during the remaining 17 years of the lease, and 12,000 of these he places in 
reserve, as being situated in localities difficult to work with much chanece of 
profit. 

Lis proposal is, therefore, to work out 34,000 T class sound green Deodar, 
at the rate of 2,000 trees per annum during the 17 remaining years of the lease ; 
indenting on the reserve of 12,000 in case the resources of these forests havo 
been over-estimated. 

8. But, besides the 2,000 I class trees which Mr. Ifcarle proposes to fell 


as above, hoe would include in the year’s feliings all dry standing and all fallen 
timber, It may be safely urged, therefore, that he will fell 86,000 I class 


Z 


trees during the 17 years, and that exclusive of existing dry material. In other 
words, he will be forced on to his reserve in any case. But, of course, there is 
just as much chance that his resources have been under-estimated. This esti- 
mate of available material must, by its nature, be exceedingly rough, excluding 
as it does all trees growing on inaccessible ground, and, éé is presumed, all trees 
that, from the character of the surrounding growth, eannot be removed during 


the next 17 years. Ur. Hearle goes into no explanalions, however, on this very 
important point. 


9. Assuming the estimates to bo accurate, we should have, by the end 
of the lease, the following | class trees on the grouna :— 


(a) 101,000 trees that are already T class, but reported to be now un- 
exploitable—but from what causes, ¢¢ is nod said. 


(0) Some 25,000 trees now belonging to the I[ class. But, here again, 
it is impossible for us to understand from the Report whether 
the present II class trees include, in all cases, accessible 
trees only. 


Tn the case of a very considerable portion of the survey, att trees were 
taken into account, however situated ; while, over the rest of the ground, no trees 
were considered that grew in localities difheult of access. 


10. Let it also be observed that, by the end of the lease, the untouched 
((.e., untouched for the next 17 years) forests of Lambatach and Datmir, agere- 
gating 3,443 acres, should furnish a considerable quota of 1 class trees. 


On the whole, it would seem, from the data before us, that the proposed 
fellings are on an ecceedingly moderate seale. 


11. But bearing in mind the difficulties and disadvantages connected with 
the working of the tracts placed by Mr. Hearle in reserve—see paragraph 120, 
page 36, of his Report—it is questionable whether we should not insist on a 
rizid adherence to the plain of operations given by him in paragraph 119. Mr. 
llearle deprecates this course; but L think the safe principle will be to insist 
upon it; at least to this extent that the areca worked over cach five years shall 
not exceel the area given for thit period in the plan of operations, and shall 
comprise the sime tracts. Unless this he done, we may find ourselves forced on 
to the reserve supply at an early stage, and then unable to work it. I think it 
inadvisable to place too much reliance ona storethat Mr. Icarle does not appear 
to be at all sure of profitably worhing. If a five-year block, as above, should 
be found to contain a six, seven, or eight years’ supply, there will, on the other 
hand, be no objection to its removal within the pe:iod corresponding (6, 7, 8 
years), 

12, T have nothing further to say on the subject of this Plan, the provi- 
sion of which may, 1 think, in other respects be accepted. It will be noticed 
that, although the Deodar trees in these forests attain to the enormous dimen- 
sions of 9 feet in girth, without deteriorating, Mr. Llearle proposes to fell all 
trees over 6 feet in girth, with the exception of apparently growing trees only 
just over that limit. Generally speaking, this would be a great mistake; but 
we are forced to this end in the present instance by the peculiar circumstances 
of the case, the principal characteristic of which is the necessity of construct. 
ing very costly and 200-mocable export-works, which can only realize a protit- 
able return so long as the fellings are concentrated ina yery great degree; in 
other words, so long as we do not spread our operations in the manner which 
the selection of a maximum development of material only would necessitate. 

ki. P. D.,—6-7-88. 

Before this Plan is passed, it must be explained what is meant by estimate 
of sound green trees available. There are apparently 137,000 I class trees, 
but only 16,000 available during the next 17 years. Why is the large balance 
unavailable ? 


I ueree in the Assistant Inspector General’s Note. 
_ B. R.,—7-7-88. 


Tehri Plan.—I should have inserted a paragraph about non-exploitable 
I class trees. From paragraph 110, it will be seen that in the Deota, Bamsa, 


ee 


3 


Sards, and Kotigad forests, every [ class tree was counted, whether growing 
on workable or unworkable ground, and whether fit for timber or the reverse ; 
whilst in the remaining forests, all trees were counted except those growing on 
precipitous, unworkable, ground. 


In these Deodar forests, as I believe in most others (vide paragraph 76 of 
Mr. D’Arey’s Plan for the Opper Ravi Leased Forests), there is a large pereent- 
ave of I class trees unfit for timber, and their proportion to the others I have 
estimated mainly from the results of the past fellings at Deota, and from the 
general quality of the trees in the other forests and the nature of the ground. 


Omitting the forests which have been worked over, and which I do not con- 
sider will be fit for ro-working before the end of the lease in 1905, we have a 
total of 114,180 1 class trees, and, out of these, £8,878, according to paragraph 
116, were available for felling, which leaves a balance of 65,302 to be account- 
ed for. I believe at least 50 per cent. of the total number of trees to be unavail- 
able, either because they are found on precipitous unworkable ground, or are 
very branchy and unfit for timber, or are overmature and unsound. The num. 
her of these trees would, therefore, be 57,090, still leaving a small balance of 
8,212, and these may have to be left for sylvicultural reasons. From paragraph 
119, it will) be seen that the number of trees to be worked out from each block 
is not rigorously fixed, entire discretion in the selection of trees being Iecft to 
the Divisional Ollicer. If, therefore, in the course of afew years it be found 
that the forests are not being worked over so rapidly as anticipated in this para- 
graph, the Conservator can easily apply for sanction to have the Plan modified 
in accordance with the further experience gained in working the forests ; but for 
the present T would not advise annually more than 2,000 being felled depart. 
mentally, and from 700 to 800 sold standing from the forests in reserve men- 
tioned in paragraph 120.
